What resolving the design found is a check at the manual too, per variant and language (`designIssues`, #554): `page-design-missing-translation`, `page-design-dropped-text` (a text in neither the manual's nor the source language, left out), `page-design-region-languages-differ` (a region whose translation has other block IDs than the source's), `page-design-missing-value` (not for the release and date, which the checks never know yet), `page-design-missing-text-attribute` and `page-design-missing-variant-attribute`, each with `params.design`, `params.at` and, where it names one, `params.name`. A manual with a callout legend beside an image (`hasLegendBeside`) is rendered for its layout too, even with `pdf.standard: none` and no design, an extra render per variant and language: a legend that does not fit the image's height even at `pdf.calloutLabelMinSize` is cut off and is a `callout-label-overflow` warning at the manual (`params.module`, `params.image`, `params.callout`), per variant and language (#548). A designed manual's safety message taller than a whole page breaks across pages instead of being kept on one, a `safety-message-split` warning at the manual (`params.module`, `params.block`, `params.text` its hazard, #668). The design screen's layout warnings leave both out.
- **Which design a manual is printed with** (#589, `DesignsInUse` in `content/core/design-in-use.ts`): a manual (`GET /manuals`, `GET /manuals/:id`) and each of a release's outputs (`GET /releases/:id/outputs`, and the retried output) carry `meta.design` (`DesignInUse`): `id` (`builtin` for the built-in design), `name` (null for the built-in design and for one that is not there), `source` (`manual`: the manifest's `design`; `project`: `pdf.design`; `builtin`: neither) and `missing` (the design named is not in the content, or its file is no design: the PDF uses the built-in one). A manual's is worked out at the ref read, an output's at the release's tag, so an older release keeps naming the design it was printed with. Each design is read once per request. A missing design is also the blocking check `unknown-page-design` at the manual (`params.design`, `params.from`: `manual` or `config`).
- **The design screen's preview** (#392): `GET …/pdf/pages/:page.png` takes `?design=<id>` (`builtin` for the built-in design) to lay a manual out with that design in place of its own (`ReadableContent.resolve`'s `design`; designs carry no audience, so this shows nothing the reader could not read), and `GET /manuals/:id/variants/:variant/:lang/pdf/layout-warnings` (`ManualLayoutWarningsAction`, paged) lists what that PDF's layout cannot fit (`LayoutWarning`: `code` `page-design-overflow` or `running-head-truncated`, `at`, `page`, `pages`, `text`), with the same `?ref=`, `?workingCopy=` and `?design=`, from the same rendering as its pages. With `?workingCopy=true` a design's working copy stands in for its file, a design made only in the change set too (`ContentOverlay.pageDesign`); the preview shows a file a page design committed at the branch names, as well as those a module the author sees uses and those uploaded into the change set, never one only a working copy names.
- **Fonts** (#555): uploaded into a change set (`POST /change-sets/:changeSetId/assets?kind=font`, "Fonts" under "Image uploads") and named by a design's `fonts.body` or `fonts.heading` (`family`, the faces as `sha256:<hash>`). A design's TrueType or OpenType face is loaded as it is, a WOFF or WOFF2 face is decoded to one in the compiling process (`@openfolia/render-typst`'s README); a font that is not stored or does not load is left out and the default font is used, and the manual's checks say so: `page-design-missing-font` (a warning, once per manual: `params.design`, `params.at` the face, `params.asset`; judged by the file's first bytes). The PDF finds a role's faces by the role, whatever `family` says (`RenderResult.fonts`, `@openfolia/render-typst`'s README): the family is what the design screen shows.
- **A manual's cover image** (#394): a manifest's `coverImage` (`default`, the manual's, and `variants`, a variant's own by its exact values) wins over the design's. A manual on the built-in design that gives a variant a cover image is laid out with the built-in design (the classic preset) so the image shows. A cover image is named like any file of a working copy (uploaded into the change set, or one the author may read: `unknown-asset` otherwise), served by `GET /assets/:sha256` to every reader of the manual (typed by its bytes, as a design's files), listed by `GET /assets` with `meta.usedInManuals`, kept in the reference index (the manifests are read whole, as the designs), so it is never unused while a manual names it. One that is not stored or is no image is left out of the PDF (the title page closes the gap) and reported: `missing-cover-image` (a warning, `params.at`, `params.asset`); a variant's own for a variant the manual does not build is `unknown-cover-variant`; an alternative text without the source language's is `missing-cover-alt` (blocking).
- **Serving** (#555): `GET /assets/:sha256` serves a file a page design at `?ref=` names (its logo, fonts, cover image, a region's images) to every reader of the ref, as the designs are, after the module rule: a file a design names is no more restricted than the design, even when a module of a narrower audience shows it too. Its type comes from its bytes, never from how it was stored: a font as `font/ttf`, `font/otf`, `font/woff` or `font/woff2`, always a download; a PNG, JPEG, GIF or WebP image in place; anything else (an SVG of the old layout too) as `application/octet-stream`, a download. Always with `nosniff`, the sandboxing CSP and the caching of every content route. `GET /assets?filter[type]=font` lists the fonts the designs at the ref name (and, with `filter[changeSet]`, the author's uploads into it), each with `attributes.font`: `format`, `family`, `subfamily`, `weight`, `italic`, and the `copyright`, `licence` and `licenceUrl` the font states (read from the whole file once and kept, `AssetInfoReader`; only for a file the blob store holds as a font: the same bytes stored as an attachment first get no facts).
- **Own templates** (#396): a design's `template` names a folder of the content repository, `templates/<id>/` with its `manual.typ` (`@openfolia/render-typst`'s README, "Own templates": the contract and the example template), which lays its PDF out in place of the preset's template (the preset still decides the title page's regions). Templates are project files reviewed in change sets: pushed to the repository, or edited in a change set as subjects of their own (`template`, below and "Subjects"). **`GET /page-design-templates`** lists them at `?ref=` (`PageDesignTemplate`: `id` the folder, `attributes.path` its entry file; paged; only the listing is read; with `workingCopy=true` the change set's author also sees a template that exists only in its working copies), with `content.read`, as designs carry no audience; `GET /page-designs/:designId` names a template the repository does not have in `meta.issues` (`unknown-template`, at `template`), and the PDF then uses the preset. `TypstPdfRenderer` reads the folder (`ReadableContent.renderSource`'s `designTemplate`, only regular files, at most 200 files and 16 MiB, judged from the listing before a file is read) and maps it into the compilation at its repository paths: a template reads its own files, the design's assets and the manual's images, never another template, a module, the configuration or anything of the host ("What a template can reach"), within the compile timeout and the memory limit (`CCMS_PDF_MEMORY_MB`, below). A template that does not compile answers **422 `template-error`** for the PDF, its pages and its layout warnings: `meta.design`, `meta.template` and `meta.problems` (each `file` as its repository path, `line`, `column`, `message`, `trace`), and is logged with them (the render log); one that needs too much memory is 422 `render-memory-exceeded`. In the checks it is **blocking**: `page-design-template-error` at every manual laid out with it, per variant and language (`params.design`, `template`, `file`, `line`, `column`, `problem`), and at the design (`{ type: "design", designId }`) in a change set that changes the design or anything in its template's folder (`changedTemplates` over the change set's paths), compiled on the renderer's own small manual (`templateProbe`, `PdfRenderer.checkDesignTemplate`), so a broken template holds the change set's merge even when no manual uses the design yet; `page-design-unknown-template` (blocking) names a template that is not there, at the manual and at a changed design. A merged commit's template is the one every reader's PDF uses, so a broken template in a change set never reaches the released PDFs until a fix is merged.
- **Editing a template's files** (#396). A template's text files (`isTemplateFilePath` and `isTemplateTextFile` of the content model: letters, digits, `_`, `.`, `-` per segment, no segment starting with a dot, at most 8 names deep and 150 characters, an extension of `.typ`, `.yaml`, `.yml`, `.json`, `.toml`, `.csv`, `.txt`, `.xml`, `.svg` or `.bib`) are each a subject `{ type: "template", templateId, path }` (`templates/<templateId>/<path>`); any other file of the folder (a font, an image) is listed but read only. All with `content.read`, at `?ref=`, and with `workingCopy=true` the change set's working copies stand in (`ReadsWorkingCopies`):
  - **`GET /page-design-templates/:templateId/files`**: its files, paged and sorted by path (`PageDesignTemplateFile`: `path`, `size`, `text` whether it is an editable text file; `meta.draft` when a working copy stands in for it); 404 for a template that is not there;
  - **`GET /page-design-templates/:templateId/file?path=`**: one text file (`attributes.text`); 404 for anything else alike: a path outside the rules (`..`, an absolute path, a hidden file, a binary extension), a file that is not there, bytes that are no UTF-8 text;
  - **`GET /page-design-templates/:templateId/preview/pages/:page.png`** (`width`, `design`): one page of the renderer's probe manual laid out with the template, the editor's live preview; a template that does not compile is 422 `template-error` with its problems, one past the last page 404 with `meta.pages`. It compiles in the renderer's sandbox as any PDF does;
  - **`GET /page-design-template-example/files`**: the renderer's example template's files as text (`{ path, text }`, paged), which the editor copies into a new template;
  - **`GET|PUT|DELETE /change-sets/:changeSetId/page-design-templates/:templateId/files/working-copy?path=`**: the working copy of one file, as any subject's (`{ content: { text } }`, at most 256 KiB of UTF-8 without NUL characters, refused past it; a path outside the rules is 404, and 400 for its lock). Its lock, working copy and save point need `content.edit` through a role whose scope restricts nothing, as a design's (`checkDesignChange`); a file is deleted by a save point's `deletes`. Saving never compiles: whether the template compiles is the preview's and the change set's check (`page-design-template-error`, at every design naming it). The audit log names each change `template.write` or `template.delete`.
- Not yet: the delivery API (designs are not part of it); editing a template's files in the editor (they are pushed to the repository).

## Chunks

The `chunks` component (`src/chunks/`, #52): `GET /chunks` gives retrieval pipelines the content as ready-made chunks, with the metadata they filter on and a hash to re-embed only what changed. Part of the delivery API.

- **Chunking** is pure, in the content model (`toChunk`, `@openfolia/content-model`, `lib/chunk.ts`): one chunk per module and language, the module as Markdown (CommonMark with GFM tables; `lib/markdown.ts`): the title as `#` heading, sections as headings one level deeper each (at most `######`), lists and procedure steps (numbered), tables as Markdown tables (a header row of empty cells when the table has none; a spanned cell's text in its first column and row; a cell's blocks joined by `<br>`), safety blocks and notes as quotes headed by the signal word or caption of the module's language (`outputLabels`, as the PDF prints them), images as `*Figure:* <title> — <alt>`, then its callouts' legends numbered as the chunk's variant shows them (`— 1 Power switch; 2 Filter`, only the callouts it shows; without a variant, every one, #546), attachments as `*Attachment:* <name> — <description>`, literal and passthrough blocks fenced, page breaks left out. Every character Markdown would read as markup or HTML is backslash-escaped, so no content becomes markup. Text attributes stay `{name}` (a module has no values of its own); a cross-reference without text shows its target's title.
- **Module references** are inlined as `resolveManual` inlines them (a missing translation falls back to the source language, a cycle is not followed), but only modules the reader may see (the loader is the reader's own modules) and no more restricted than the including module (an audience above its own, or not configured, is left out), so a chunk's `audience` holds for all of its text.
- **Variants:** with `filter[variant]` (a variant slug, `model=large,software=2.3`; checked against the configuration like the module preview's `?variant=`, else 422 `unknown-variant`), variant blocks are resolved for it, a test on an attribute it does not set not holding. Without it, each variant block is a quote headed by `**when**` and its condition. `conditions` lists the variant conditions in the text either way (`when` text, document order, each once).
- **FAQ entries** (#151): each FAQ entry in a module has a chunk of its own after the module's (`toChunks`), in document order: `id` `<lang>/<moduleId>/<blockId>`, `attributes.entry` (`blockId`, `question` as plain text), the entry alone as `text` (the question as `#` heading, then its answer), its own `sourceHash`, `conditions` (the variant blocks around it first) and `meta.sources`; the other attributes are the module's, and the module's chunk keeps the entries in its text. With `filter[variant]`, an entry in a variant block that does not hold for it is left out before paging. An entry a module reference brings in is that module's.
- **`Chunk`**: `id` `<lang>/<moduleId>` (an FAQ entry's: `<lang>/<moduleId>/<blockId>`); `attributes` `moduleId`, `lang`, `title`, `moduleType`, `audience`, `facets`, `keywords`, `manuals` (the manuals that include the module: their manifests name it, as the reader may read them, within their audience), `conditions`, `text`, `sourceHash` (SHA-256 of the module's translation units' source hashes by block ID, or of its canonical AsciiDoc when it has none), `contentHash` (SHA-256 of `text`); `relationships.module` with `links.related` (the module at the commit read); `meta` `ref`, `commit`, `sources` (the module files the text was made from, the module first) and `release` (`meta.status.release` of the module routes: the newest release tag holding it, `GetModuleStatusesAction`). The text is made from the parsed module, so a change in the file's formatting alone (or in block IDs) changes neither `text` nor `contentHash` (a test holds it).
- **Filters** run before paging, through `ReadableContent`, so a chunk outside the reader's scope is never listed, counted or inlined: `filter[module]` (one module ID: its chunks, one per language the reader may read it in; one outside the scope has none, like one that does not exist), `filter[lang]`, the module filters shared with `GET /search` (`filter[type]`, `filter[audience]`, `filter[facet]=<facet>:<value>`, `filter[keyword]`, the last two repeatable), `filter[manual]` (the modules the manual includes, as the reader may read it; with `filter[variant]`, the entries it includes for that variant; a manual the reader may not see includes nothing, like one that does not exist) and `filter[changedSince]`. Ordered by module ID, then language.
- **`filter[changedSince]=<commit>`** (a full commit ID in the history of the commit read, `ContentChanges`, port in `core/`, adapter `GitContentChanges` over `GitRepo.isAncestor` and `changedPaths`; else 400 `invalid-parameter`, so a draft commit says nothing to a reader of `main`): only the chunks that may differ since: a module file their text was made from changed (theirs or one they include), a file the reader may see now of a module their text depends on changed or appeared (a translation of an included module, a module a reference names that was missing or too restricted, a cross-reference target whose title the text shows; `Chunk.dependsOn` in the content model), the configuration changed (every chunk), or a manifest naming the module changed. `meta.release` is no file: a new release tag lists nothing here, so refresh it with a full sync after a release. Only files the reader may read now count (`meta.sources`, the manifests they may read), so nothing is told of a file outside their scope; in turn a chunk whose included module was deleted, lost its translation or left the reader's scope since, a manual that stopped naming a module, a deleted module and a change of the reader's own scope are not listed: a full sync now and then catches them. The history of the commit read is content the reader may already read, so that commit is never opened.
- **Refs:** `?ref=` like every content read; drafts (any ref other than `main` and tags) only with `content.read.draft`, so a consumer token never reads one. `meta.release` depends on the tags too, so the route is `@BeyondTheCommit()` (ETag a hash of the answer, `private, no-cache`).
- **Cache:** the chunks of a commit are made once per reading key (every variant block kept) and kept for the last `CHUNK_SETS_KEPT` (8) of them (`Recent`), and for at most `CHUNK_SETS_KEPT_CHARS` (250,000,000) characters together, as the modules that got chunks counted them (#684: a module left out keeps nothing and weighs nothing; the least recently used dropped first, the newest kept whatever it holds; two sets of the scale generator's `xl` profile, eight of `large`); for `filter[variant]` only the page's chunks are made again for the variant, so a variant costs no more than its page.
- **Ingest recipe:** page through `GET /chunks?ref=main` (keep the first page's `X-Ccms-Commit` and read the other pages at `?ref=<that commit>`, so every page comes from one commit); store each chunk by `id`, embedding it only when its `contentHash` is new; filter retrieval on `audience`, `facets`, `manuals`, `lang` and `meta.release`. Next time, `GET /chunks?ref=main&filter[changedSince]=<the commit kept>` lists only what to re-check; keep the new `X-Ccms-Commit`. A full sync now and then (and after each release, for `meta.release`) drops chunks whose modules were deleted or left the scope. To learn what to drop as well (deleted, deprecated, withdrawn or no longer in scope), follow the change feed instead (`GET /changes`, "Change feed").
- **The expanded size** (#681): a chunk's references are walked as a manual's ("The expanded size" below; `toChunks` in the content model): each module read once per chunk however many paths lead to it, and the chunks of one module (its own and its FAQ entries', counted together, so a module of many large entries reaches the limits sooner than its manual would) held to `MAX_EXPANDED_NODES`, `MAX_EXPANDED_CHARS` and `MAX_NESTING_DEPTH`, counted over what the reader may see, with the titles cross-references show, the Markdown written around nested blocks and each table's grid (a cell per row and column, before it is made) counted too. Each module is cut on a count of its own, so one module past a limit never takes another's budget. **Past one, only that module is left out** (#688): it has no chunk (nor its FAQ entries), it is not counted in `meta.page.total`, and every other module's chunks are made as usual; `meta.leftOut` names it (`moduleId`, `lang`, `reason` `size` or `depth`), on every page of a request whose filters ask for it (all but `filter[changedSince]`, which a left-out module has no chunk to compare for), in module ID, then language order, and is absent when nothing is left out. Only a module the reader may see can be left out, so neither the list nor the counts tell anything of a hidden one; the set, what it left out included, is kept per commit and reading key like any (`CHUNK_SETS_KEPT`). With `filter[variant]`, each module of the page is made once for the variant, its chunk and its entries' together; one past a limit for that variant alone (a hidden callout's `[callout]` can be longer than its legend) is left out of the page and named in `meta.leftOut` of that page only, and stays counted in `meta.page.total` (the set it is paged from holds it), so that page has fewer items than `page[size]`. The change feed carries such a release without that module's chunks (a chunk a reader held of it is removed as `gone`). Before release, the module's own checks report it: a blocking `expansion-too-large` at the module (`params.reason`), made as the chunks are, over what the reader may see ("Checks"); a checks run's cuts share one budget (#711, "Checks"). A set is kept per commit and reading key, so two readers of different scopes never share one. **The limits in tests** (#726): `ListChunksAction` and `ContentChecks` take the limits they cut to through `EXPANSION_LIMITS_TOKEN` (`chunks/core/expansion-limits.ts`, optional, checked to be whole numbers from 1 up to the real limits, so it can only lower them). No module of the app provides it, so they always cut to the content model's `EXPANSION_LIMITS`, and neither a setting nor a request can change them; only `createTestApp({}, { expansionLimits })` and a unit test's constructor give smaller ones, so the chunk specs cross the same boundaries with content about a hundred times smaller.
- **The set as a whole** (#684): every module is held to the limits on its own, but many modules each just under them (a hundred small modules referring to one that multiplies) would expand to over a billion characters together. So the chunks of a commit, as one reader sees them, share one budget, counted with the same counters as each module (`ExpansionBudget` in the content model): `MAX_CHUNK_SET_FACTOR` (4) times the content they are made from (the summed nodes, characters and table cells of the modules the reader may see), plus room for `CHUNK_SET_MODULES_AT_LIMITS` (32) modules at the limits of one, so the modules one slip makes too large (#688) leave out only themselves. A module left out spends what it walked before it stopped. The text the set keeps (what the modules that get chunks spent) is held apart, to the same four times the content's characters plus room for `CHUNK_SET_KEPT_AT_LIMITS` (8) modules at `MAX_EXPANDED_CHARS`, so a commit of little content keeps at most 96,000,000 characters however much work its left-out modules took. The scale generator's `xl` profile (77,000 module files across 8 languages) expands to 1.4 times its content, so it stays far within; its chunks are unchanged. **Past the budget, the set stops there** and every request for it (each page, each filter, `filter[variant]` and `filter[changedSince]` too) is 422 `chunk-set-too-large` (`meta.nodes`, `meta.chars`, `meta.tableCells`: the budget, `chars` the room for the kept text when that was passed, with `meta.kept: true`; `meta.largest`: the up to five modules, among those the reader may see, whose chunks expanded the most so far, `moduleId`, `lang`, `chars`, `nodes`; `chunks/core/chunk-set-too-large.ts`). The refusal is kept like a set (it weighs nothing), so asking again costs no work. With `filter[variant]`, each module's text for the variant is held to a budget of its own (`variantBudget`: twice what it spent without one, plus `VARIANT_SLACK`), so a page costs about twice its own text, never the set, and a module past it is left out of that page alone, as one past a limit for the variant (never another module of the page). Refused rather than cut down: which modules to leave out could only be told by doing the work the budget stops, and leaving out the modules after an arbitrary point would serve a partial set as if it were whole, so a sync would drop good chunks; a refusal is clear, and is the commit's (and reader's) only: a reader who may not see the modules that multiply is served, and every other commit is untouched. Before release, a change set's checks report it (#710): its head's set, as the reviewer sees it, is cut as here (`ListChunksAction.tooLarge`), and past the budget each module of `meta.largest` gets a blocking `chunk-set-too-large` ("Checks"); the change feed skips a release whose set is too large, as one that cannot be read ("Change feed"), and the agent server answers `chunk-set-too-large` as it gets it.
- Out of scope: push delivery (#42). The search's own embeddings (#54, "Postgres search") are not delivered here. The agent server (#53, `backend/mcp`) reads modules through this route (`read_module` is `filter[module]` and `filter[lang]`).

## Derived manuals

A derived manual (#451, `kind: derived` with a `rule`, `@openfolia/content-model`'s README, "Derived manuals"), such as a safety leaflet, has its entries gathered from its source manual whenever it is read: `DerivedManuals` (`content/core/derived-manuals.ts`) wraps the snapshot `ReadableContent` reads (and the module index builds from), so `GET /manuals`, `GET /manuals/:id` (its `kind` and `rule` in the attributes, `entries` the gathered ones, front matter first), its checks, PDFs and pages, translation status, the release outputs and their changes, the DITA and iiRDS exports and the module index read it as any manual, and it follows its source at every commit: a change to a gathered module, or a module that turns safety, shows in the leaflet's next release and its changes list. Gathered from what the reader's snapshot holds (with a change set's drafts under a live preview, from the drafts), and only from a source manual the reader may see (one they may not is, for them, a source that does not exist: no entries, text attributes or issues from it, and `unknown-source-manual` worded alike for a missing, a broken and a hidden source; `DerivedManuals.of(raw, sees)`, one gathering per reader). A derived manual is for its source's audience or a narrower one: for a wider one it gathers nothing, for any reader or output (`derived-audience-wider`, blocking), so the source's text never reaches readers it is not for and a reader of a derived manual always sees its source. The source's modules are then read whole for their types (32 at a time), and the result filtered like any manifest: entries and the rule's lists (`front`, `exclude`, `back`) name only modules the reader may see (a module hidden from them is an `outside-scope` note, not a name), and so do the issues gathering finds; `empty-derived-manual` is told per reader (nothing gathered they may see), so a gathered module hidden from them does not show by its absence. Resolving it takes the source's text attributes under its own (`ReadableContent.textAttributesOf`; the DITA export's keys too); its stored attributes stay its own. A working copy holds the gathered entries for showing; a save point prints the rule, never the entries (`StoredManifest` holds the file to it). An earlier manifest of it (its history) is gathered from the commit read; its revision (`manualHistory`, `manualRevision`) hashes it without the gathered entries, so a revision read here and at its own commit (a release's) match, and a change to its source alone is no version of it. Saving a derived manual's working copy refuses a rule whose source manual the author may not read (403 `derived-source-outside-scope`; a missing or broken source too, unless they read manuals of every audience: a hidden and a missing one answer alike). The module index never carries a derived manual over from another commit: its entries depend on other files. What gathering finds is in the manual's `issues` and its checks: `unknown-source-manual`, `derived-source`, `no-gathered-types` and `derived-audience-wider` (blocking), `safety-module-excluded`, `unknown-excluded-module` and `empty-derived-manual` (warnings; "Checks"). Which module types count as safety is configuration only (`moduleTypes[].safety`); a rule's own `moduleTypes` override it. A rule's `notices` (#670, `@openfolia/content-model`'s README, "Gathering safety notices") gathers the source's safety messages in place of whole modules, grouped by source module or by hazard level: the source's modules are then read with the modules they refer to (32 at a time), its entries are the modules holding notices, and resolving it gives their notices only, so everything above reads it as any manual (the PDF and pages, the portal, release outputs and their changes list, translation status and the XLIFF export, which follow each gathered module whole, as its translation does). Only from what the reader may see: it is gathered per reader from the modules they may see (`DerivedManuals.of(raw, sees, seesModule)`, `seesModule` the manifest's `#mayName`), so a module they may see whose only notice is in one they may not is not gathered for them, nor named by `safety-notice-excluded`; resolving leaves out what they may not see as anywhere; and where a module is used (the module index, translation status) takes such a manual's modules from their own gathering (`ReadableContent.noticeManualModules`), not the index's, gathered for everyone. The iiRDS package gives a module once per topic, with its notices of every level; a DITA package of it is refused (422 `dita-notices-unsupported`): DITA topics are whole modules.

## The expanded size

A manual's module references are followed once per resolution (#679, `@openfolia/content-model`'s README, "Manuals and variants"): each module is read once, whatever the paths to it, and a manual gathering safety notices searches each module once. A resolution is made per reader (what they may see) and never shared between readers. A module referred to over several paths still appears once per path, so references that multiply (two modules referring to the same one, both referred to by one, and so on many levels down) make a manual too large to make: past `MAX_EXPANDED_NODES` (500,000 content nodes) or `MAX_EXPANDED_CHARS` (12,000,000 characters), each included module's counted per inclusion (over six times the largest manual measured, so not a setting), or with blocks and references nested deeper than `MAX_NESTING_DEPTH` (100), resolving stops at once and gives nothing. Every route that resolves it answers 422 `expansion-too-large` (`meta.nodes`, `meta.chars`, `meta.tableCells`, `meta.depth`: the limits; `manuals/core/too-large.ts`): the resolved manual, its PDF, pages, layout warnings, HTML, hidden content and iiRDS packages, a module's preview and hidden content, and a derived manual gathering it; a release output records the code. Its checks list it as one blocking `expansion-too-large` check per variant and language at the manual, and skip its PDF check. A module's own checks (in a manual's checks and a change set's) report `expansion-too-large` at the module when its retrieval chunks pass the limits (#688): they keep every variant block, so a module can pass in each variant of its manual and still be too large to cut. Counted only over what the reader may see: a reader who may not see the modules that multiply it may read it. Retrieval chunks are held to the same limits (#681, "Chunks").

## Table caps

A table has at most 1,000 columns and covers at most 50,000 cells (rows × columns), and the tables of a module, or of a page design's regions, 1,000,000 cells together (#686, `@openfolia/content-model`'s README, "Table caps"). One past them is refused before any grid is laid out: a working-copy save (422 `table-too-large`, "Working copies"), an import (its item refused with `table-too-large`, so the import cannot be applied). A module file already in Git holding one is not read: reading or previewing it is 422 `table-too-large` (`meta.path`, `meta.columns`, `meta.cells`, `meta.totalCells`), and a manual including it resolves and renders without it, as any module whose file cannot be read (`invalid-module`, a blocking check). A table past the caps that reaches a renderer or the DITA export anyway (it never should: content is parsed and validated first) is the same 422, never a 500 (`ApiExceptionFilter`).

## Titles and labels per language

A manual's title, its groups' titles (`manuals/<id>.yaml`) and every configured label (`openfolia.yaml`: module types, audiences, facets and their values, variant attributes and their values, text attributes, safety symbols, callout styles) are a `LocalizedText` (#731, `@openfolia/content-model`'s README): one text for every language, or one per language tag, the source language's among them. A language without its own takes the source language's.

- **Stored form in `attributes`**: `GET /config` (`ProjectConfig`, with its `translations` record), a `Manual`'s `title` and its groups' `entries[].title`, and `GET /variants`' labels give the text exactly as stored, one text or the map, so a client that edits them writes back what it read. A working copy of the configuration is checked with `StrictProjectConfig` (a map without the source language is 422; read leniently from Git, the source text is then taken from its first language) and a manifest's by `validateManifest` (`missing-source-title`, blocking, and `unknown-language` for a language that is neither the source nor a target language, `params.at` `title`, a group's source title or `<…>.<lang>`).
- **Resolved where a route takes a language**: the resolved manual (`GET /manuals/:id/variants/:variant/:lang`: `title`, group titles), its PDF (title page, running header, outline and table of contents, the page design's `title`, `variant` and `audience` placeholders, the variant subtitle, `variantLabel`), its HTML preview (the subtitle, safety symbols' text alternatives, text attribute names), the iiRDS package of a release output (the audience's, facets' and variant values' `rdfs:label`), a stable address opened in a language (a withdrawn release's title) give the text in that language, else the source language's (`textIn`), as module text falls back.
- **In the source language** where a listing has no language: a `Manual`'s `meta.variants[].label`, `GET /variants/matches`' `meta`, the checks, search, the module index (manuals' titles where a module is used), the translation status and the coverage report (`meta.title`), assets' `usedInManuals`, a stable address's language picker.

## Variants

The `variants` component (#69), for the editor's variant picker and the variants admin (#91). Its routes read like every content route (`content.read`, `?ref=`, `X-Ccms-Commit`, ETag per commit and reading key) and are paged.

- **`GET /variants`**: one `VariantAttribute` per configured variant attribute (`variantAttributes` in `openfolia.yaml`), in the configured order; `id` is the attribute's ID, `attributes` its `kind`, `label` and, for an `enum` attribute, its `values` (`{ id, label }`, configured order; each `label` as stored, one text or one per language, see "Titles and labels per language"; `archived: true` on a value archived in the configuration, which content may still use but a picker no longer offers). A `version` attribute has no values: it is compared numerically. Nothing here is hardcoded; it is the configuration as it stands at the ref.
- **`GET /variants/matches?when=<condition>`**: the builds a condition holds for. A build is a manual in one of the variant combinations its manifest lists (`variants:`), so `meta.page.total` is the "appears in N builds" count. One `VariantBuild` each (`id` `<manual ID>/<variant slug>`, `attributes.values`, the `manual` relationship, `meta` with the `slug` the manual routes take, the `label` and the manual's title, both in the source language), in the order `GET /manuals` lists the manuals, then in the manifest's order. Evaluated with the content model's `evaluateCondition`, as resolving a manual evaluates a variant block: versions compare numerically (`2.10 > 2.9`, `2.3 = 2.3.0`; `in` on a version attribute is numeric equality), and a test on an attribute a combination does not set does not hold, not even negated.
- **`GET /variants/:attributeId/usages`** (#91): where a variant attribute is named, or with `filter[value]=<value ID>` one of its values: what the variants admin lists before a value is archived or removed. One `VariantUse` each (`id` `manual/<manual ID>` or `module/<module ID>`; `attributes.kind`, the `manualId` or `moduleId`, the `title` (a module's from its source-language file, else its first) and for a module the `languages` whose files name it), manuals first (their variant combinations, the conditions of their entries and text attributes), then modules (their variant blocks), each by title, then ID. Read from the module index (`variants` on its rows), so only what the reader may see is listed or counted (`ReadableModuleIndex`): a manual's entry condition counts only where the entry covers a module the reader may name, as the manifest served to them leaves out the others, so a manual is never listed for a condition on a part of it they cannot see. Nothing is checked against the configuration: a value it no longer holds is still found where content names it. An ID that is no configuration key is 400 `invalid-parameter`.
- **`when` syntax**: the text a variant block's `when="…"` takes in AsciiDoc, which is what the editor's variant block shows, read by the content model's `parseCondition`: `model in (large, small)`, `software >= 2.3` (`<`, `<=`, `>`, `>=`), `!(…)`, `&&` (binds tighter than `||`), `||` and parentheses; a value that is not letters, digits, `.`, `_` and `-` goes in single quotes (`'a b'`, `\'` and `\\` inside). At most 1,000 characters. Missing, repeated, longer or not parsing: 400 `invalid-parameter` (`source.parameter: when`) whose `detail` is the parser's message. Parsing but naming an attribute or value the configuration does not hold, comparing an enum attribute with `<`, or comparing a version attribute with something that is not a dotted version (`software >= abc`; `invalid-version-value`, #225): 422 `unknown-variant`, one error per problem with the validation code in `meta.issue` (`unknown-variant-attribute`, `unknown-variant-value`, `invalid-version-value`, `version-compare-on-enum`), as `GET /modules/:lang/:id/preview?variant=` answers a variant the configuration does not hold.
- **Scope**: only the manuals the reader may read count (`ReadableContent.manuals()`), so a manual outside the reader's scope is never named, listed or counted; a manifest that cannot be read lists no build. The variant combinations of a manual the reader may read are manual-level data, as in `GET /manuals/:id`.

## Users, roles and permissions

The `access` component (`src/access/`). Permissions are code; roles are data: a role is a name, a set of permissions and a scope, so new roles need no code change.

- **Permissions** (`core/permissions.ts`): `content.read` (released content: `main` and release tags), `content.read.draft` (everything else), `content.edit`, `content.review`, `content.approve`, `release.publish`, `translation.manage`, `webhook.manage`, `admin.roles`, `admin.branding` (#169: the platform's branding), `admin.backups` (#529: the instance's backups and their key). The last four are global: a scope does not apply to them.
- **A new permission reaches existing installations through its migration.** Roles are rows in Postgres (`roles.permissions`), and the seed writes only when it is run by hand, so a permission added to `PERMISSIONS` would otherwise be held by no one after an upgrade, and the screen it guards could be opened by no one until someone edits a role. So the issue's migration ends with a data statement granting it to every role that holds its closest existing permission (the one whose holders could already do or grant what it allows), audited as `role.update` by `service:migration`, and the PR names that choice. `admin.branding` went to the holders of `admin.roles` (`0043_branding`: an admin who can define roles could grant it to themselves anyway, so it adds no power), tested by migrating a database seeded before it (`src/branding/repos/branding-upgrade.spec.ts`). `admin.backups` went to the holders of `admin.roles` too (`0046_backups`, for the same reason; `src/backups/repos/backups-upgrade.spec.ts`). Each such test migrates only up to its own migration, so a later grant does not change it.
- **Scope** (`core/actor.ts`): each dimension optional, missing meaning all of it: `languages`, `moduleTypes`, `maxAudience` (that audience level and every less restricted one) and `facets` (per facet, the values covered, e.g. `{ "product-line": ["tuggers"], "region": ["eu"] }`: product lines, regions or whatever the content configuration classifies by). Values are the configuration's IDs (`openfolia.yaml`); one it does not know matches nothing.
- **`allows(actor, permission, target)`** (`core/allows.ts`), pure: some role of the actor holds the permission and the target lies within that same role's scope (roles never combine into a wider grant). The target is a module's language, type, audience and facets with the configuration's audience order (`moduleTarget(meta, config)`), or `ANYWHERE` (whether the permission is held at all, e.g. to offer a button). It fails closed: a module without an audience is the most restricted level (internal, by default), an audience level not configured is outside every `maxAudience`, a dimension the scope restricts but the target leaves out is outside it. Facets differ between reading and changing: a read needs the module to name none of the facet's values (it applies to all) or share one with the scope; any other permission needs at least one value and all of them in the scope, so a change never reaches content outside it.
- **Tables** (`repos/access.tables.ts`): `users` (`id`, `name`, `email` (optional, unique ignoring case), `deactivated_at`), `roles` (`id`, `name`, `description`, `permissions`, and the scope as `languages`, `module_types`, `max_audience`, `facets`; null for all, never an empty list; IDs in the formats `RoleId` and `UserId` check, in the database too) and `role_assignments` (a role still assigned cannot be deleted; deleting a user removes its assignments). `UserDirectory.findActor(id)` gives a user with its roles (`findUser`, `listUsers`: names and role IDs); a stored role with an unknown permission or a malformed scope makes it throw (`InvalidStoredDataError`, a 500), never a partial actor.
- **Who is calling** (`api/actor.guard.ts`, `ActorGuard`, global): on every route not marked `@Public()`, it checks the token, then resolves the actor once (`ResolveActorAction`) and sets `request.actor`; a controller reads it with `@CurrentActor()` (`base/api/current-actor.decorator.ts`, 401 when there is none) and passes it into its action, so `core/` never reads HTTP. An action that refuses throws `ForbiddenError` (403 `forbidden`). A failing lookup (database down, an invalid stored role) is an error, never a fallback to another actor. An e2e test (`tests/access.e2e.spec.ts`) fails for a route that is neither public nor guarded; a new public route is added to `PUBLIC_ROUTES` (`tests/routes.ts`).
- **Permission checks** (`core/policy.ts`): every action declares its `policy`, the permission it needs, `any-actor` (only an actor, e.g. `GET /me`), or, for an action that acts for nobody, `public` (the health check) or `internal` (resolving the actor). An action acting for someone extends `ActorAction` and implements `run`; the inherited `handle` checks the policy (`allows(actor, permission, ANYWHERE)`: some role holds it) before `run` reads or writes anything, and refuses with `ForbiddenError` (403 `forbidden`) otherwise, also when there is no actor. The compiler requires `policy`; `policy.spec.ts` finds every `*.action.ts` and fails for an action that declares none, overrides `handle`, or does anything before refusing an actor without its permission. Content routes open the ref through `OpenSnapshotAction` (`content.read`), so an actor who may not read learns nothing, not even which refs exist; a route that reads content therefore needs `content.read`, whatever else its action needs. Where the target's scope matters, the action checks the loaded target too (`allows(actor, permission, moduleTarget(…))`); a **read outside the actor's scope is 404 `not-found`**, the same answer as for an item that does not exist, so the answer does not tell a reader what lies outside their scope; a change the actor may read but not make is 403. **Reads are filtered by scope** (see "Reading scope" below). A controller documents its action's permission with `@ApiPermission(permission)` (`api/api-permission.decorator.ts`): the 403 and `x-required-permission` in the OpenAPI document. `access.e2e.spec.ts` walks every guarded route: an actor without roles is refused 403 `forbidden` (except `ANY_ACTOR_ROUTES`, `tests/routes.ts`), and an actor holding only the documented permission reaches the action (with well-formed parameters).
- **Reading scope** (`content/core/readable-content.ts`). `OpenSnapshotAction` gives actions a `ReadableContent`, never the repository's snapshot: every read through it is filtered by the actor's roles holding the read permission, which is `content.read` on a released ref and `content.read.draft` on any other. `reads.spec.ts` fails for code other than the port, its adapter and the filter that names the unfiltered snapshot, and for any other place that makes a `ReadableContent`, so a new read cannot skip the filter. Code that needs a ref by name but reads no content looks it up through `ReadableRefs`, which applies the same draft rule (`readPermissionAt`).
  - **On a draft** only the scopes of the roles holding `content.read.draft` count, also where they are wider than those holding `content.read`.
  - **Released refs** (`content/core/released.ts`): the commit the branch `main` or a tag points to (every tag counts as a release: the API makes tags only as releases, `POST /releases`, and a tag made outside it counts too). Decided by commit, not by the name asked for. Any other ref or commit, also an earlier commit of `main`, needs `content.read.draft` (held in any scope); without it, it is 404 with the same detail as a ref that does not exist. `GET /refs` lists only `main` and the tags without it.
  - **Authors always see their own work** (#126): a change set's branch, named exactly (`?ref=change-sets/<id>`), opens for the change set's author without `content.read.draft` when the change set started from released content (`base_released`: one started from a draft, while its author held `content.read.draft`, holds others' unreleased work, so it stops opening for them once that permission is gone; decided once, at creation), read through the scopes of their roles holding `content.read`, as a released ref is (`AuthoredBranches`, `content/core/authored-branches.ts`, answered from the change sets). Never a commit by its ID, never another author's branch: those stay 404 like a ref that does not exist. A holder of `content.read.draft` reads it as a draft, as before.
  - **Modules**: within some role's scope (`allows`; a scope on a facet the configuration does not define matches nothing), else not there, exactly like a missing one (lists, single reads, resolved manuals, PDFs). A module without an audience is the most restricted level, so it is invisible to anyone limited below it. A file that cannot be read is listed or reported (path and error) only to an actor who may read everything in its language (`allowsAllIn`).
  - **Manuals**: listed and read when some role's `maxAudience` covers the manifest's audience (none: the most restricted level) (`allowsManual`); module types and facets decide which of its modules a reader sees, not the manual. Its manifest (`GET /manuals`, `GET /manuals/:id`) names only the modules the reader may see in some language (a reference to a module that exists in no language is kept, as it hides nothing); groups left empty go too, so `entries` can be empty. Its validation issues are those of what is left, and holders of `content.read.draft` get an `outside-scope` issue per module left out (`at`: its ID). A resolved manual's issues name no module left out of the manifest either (only that `outside-scope` issue). Titles, variants, text attributes and the conditions of what is left are manual-level data, shown to every reader of the manual. A broken manifest only for one who covers every level. Resolving (and the PDF) also needs the language in that role's scope (else 404, like a language that is not configured); modules the reader may not see are left out (a translation the reader may not see counts as missing, so a visible source version stands in, as for a missing one; a source fallback outside the scope is left out too). Holders of `content.read.draft` get an `outside-scope` issue per module left out; for anyone else a module left out and a missing one both go unreported, so they look alike.
  - **Assets**: only an image a module the reader may see refers to (else 404, like a missing one, checked before the blob store is asked). The PDF renderer reads only the images, symbols and template of the manual resolved for the reader.
  - **Lists** are filtered before paging: `meta.page.total` counts only what the reader may see.
  - **Caching**: the content ETag includes a key of what the reader may see (`readingKey`: the scopes of the roles holding the read permission, and whether it holds `content.read.draft`), so a reader never gets a 304 for another scope's response; responses carry `Vary: Authorization, X-Ccms-User`. The ETag names no route, so a reader's ETag from one route matches on every other route at that commit: before answering a 304 without running the action, `SnapshotInterceptor` checks the permission the route documents (`@ApiPermission`, kept as route metadata), so an actor without it gets 403 with or without `If-None-Match` (#365). `@BeyondTheCommit()` routes, the unread count and Express's own ETags compare after the action ran, so after its policy; the preview stylesheets and fonts have no action (the same bytes for everyone). So a route on the commit ETag whose action checks more than its documented permission (a permission on a particular target inside `run`) must be `@BeyondTheCommit()`. A 304 for an item outside the reader's scope stays a 304, as for one that does not exist: the ETag names no item, so it tells nothing. PDFs are cached by commit, manual, variant, language, that key and a hash of the resolved manual, so two readers share a rendering only when they see the same.
- **`GET /me`**: `attributes` hold the stored data (`kind`, `name`, `roles` with their scopes); `meta.permissions` the derived effective permissions: a list of `{ permission, scopes }`, each permission held with the scopes it is held in (`[{}]` for everywhere, always for global ones). For clients deciding what to offer; actions still check the target with `allows`.
- **A user's permissions** (`User` resources of `GET /users`, `GET /users/:userId` and `GET /demo/users`): `meta.permissions`, derived from its roles, lists every permission some role of theirs holds, in any scope (`heldPermissions`), so a client can tell, e.g., a contributor (#137: neither `content.review` nor `content.approve`) without a stored flag; actions still check the target.
- **`GET /demo/users`**: the users `X-Ccms-User` may name (the `User` resources of `GET /users`, paged), for a demo client's user switcher. Any actor may call it, the token's service too, since the switcher is used before anyone is chosen; in demo mode that shows nothing the shared token's holder could not read by acting as an admin. A service account's token cannot act as a user (`X-Ccms-User` is refused with it), yet it lists them too, with their `meta.permissions` (what their roles hold): accepted, as demo mode is for trying the platform out, never for real people's accounts. Outside demo mode it is 404 `not-found`: no demo route exists there, and a client takes the 404 as "demo mode is off".
- **Administering access** (#39; `access-admin.module.ts`, `api/access-admin.controller.ts`, port `AccessAdmin`, adapter `DrizzleAccessAdmin`): roles (`GET`/`POST /roles`, `GET`/`PATCH`/`DELETE /roles/:roleId`; a role has a `description`, and `meta.holders` counts its users) and users (`GET /users/:userId`, `POST /users` with an ID the client chooses, a name, an email address (unique ignoring case) and roles; `PATCH /users/:userId` changes name, email and, with `relationships.roles`, replaces the roles; `POST`/`DELETE /users/:userId/relationships/roles` add or remove some). All need `admin.roles` and apply at once, from the next request of whoever they concern. Every change runs in one transaction that first takes an advisory lock (`AccessAdmin.lock`), so access changes happen one at a time, and checks the actor again in the database (`beginAccessChange`): an admin whose role was taken, or who was deactivated, while a request was under way is refused 403. **The last admin:** a change that leaves no active user holding a role with `admin.roles` (taking the role, changing the role, deactivating, also oneself) is 409 `last-admin` and rolled back. A role still held cannot be deleted (409 `role-in-use`, `meta.holders`). Other codes: `role-exists`, `user-exists`, `email-taken`, `unknown-role` (422, `meta.roles`), `id-mismatch` (a body's `data.id` is not the path's). **Audited:** `role.create`, `role.update` (the fields changed and the grant), `role.delete`, `role.assign` / `role.unassign` (subject the role, `data.userId`), `user.create` and `user.update` (IDs and the names of the fields changed only, never a name or an email address), `user.deactivate`, `user.reactivate`.
- **Deactivating a user** (`POST /users/:userId/deactivate`): `users.deactivated_at` is set; `findActor` gives no actor for it, so its requests are 403 `user-deactivated` (the guard tells it from `unknown-user`), approvals it gave stop holding (as for an approver who lost the role), and `GET /demo/users` no longer lists it. Every lock it holds is released in the same transaction (`lock.release`, `reason` `user.deactivate`; `meta.locksReleased`). Its roles, change sets and comments stay; `POST /users/:userId/reactivate` lets it act again. Users are not deleted. Every session the user holds ends in the same transaction (the audit event's `data.sessionsEnded`), so a signed-in browser is refused at its next request. Group mapping from an identity provider is under "Sign-in" below; service accounts are below.
- **Service accounts and tokens** (#38; `api/service-accounts.controller.ts`, port `ServiceAccountStore`, adapter `DrizzleServiceAccountStore`, tables in `repos/service-accounts.tables.ts`). Machine clients (a portal build, a retrieval pipeline, an agent, CI cutting releases) act as themselves, with roles of their own: a service account is an ID (`UserId`'s format), a name, a description and roles; as an actor it is `{ kind: 'service', id }`, and every comparison of actors (authors, lock holders, approvers) checks the kind, so it never stands in for a user of the same ID. Administered like users, by people holding `admin.roles` (`beginAccessChange` refuses any actor that is not an active user, so **a service account cannot change access**, and never counts as the last admin). Least privilege: **no service account holds a role with `admin.roles`** (422 `admin-role-for-service-account` when giving one; 409 `role-held-by-service-account` when adding `admin.roles` to a role one holds), so a machine never reads users' email addresses or the audit log, nor breaks locks. Approving and signing off are a person's: a service account is 403 `forbidden` there, whatever its roles; every change is audited (`service-account.create`, `.update` (the fields changed), `.deactivate`, `.reactivate`, `role.assign` / `role.unassign` with `data.serviceAccountId`, `service-token.issue` and `service-token.revoke` with `data.tokenId`). A role a service account holds counts in the role's `meta.holders` and cannot be deleted. Deactivating one releases its locks, as for a user.
  - **Tokens** (`core/service-token.ts`): `ccms_sat_<id>_<secret>`, the ID 16 hex digits (64 bits, public: lists and the audit log name it) and the secret 64 hex digits (256 bits) from the CSPRNG; the fixed prefix lets a secret scanner recognise a leaked one (`^ccms_sat_[0-9a-f]{16}_[0-9a-f]{64}$`). `POST /service-accounts/:id/tokens` (a `name`, an optional `expiresAt`) answers the token **once**, in `meta.token` (`Cache-Control: no-store`); only its SHA-256 is stored (`service_tokens.secret_hash`), never the token; no list, log or audit event holds the token, and none but the database the hash. Every token expires: by default after 90 days, at most 366 (422 `invalid-expiry`); an account holds at most 10 tokens neither revoked nor expired (409 `too-many-tokens`), and a deactivated one gets none (409 `service-account-deactivated`). The list (`GET …/tokens`) shows each token's name, creation, expiry, last use (kept to the minute) and revocation.
  - **Authentication** (`AuthenticateServiceTokenAction`, in `ActorGuard`): a bearer token with the prefix is looked up by its ID and its hash compared in constant time (against a dummy hash when there is no such token), on every request, uncached: **revoking a token or deactivating its account applies to the very next request**. A wrong, unknown or malformed token is 401 `unauthorized`; a revoked or expired one 401 `token-revoked` / `token-expired`, said only to a caller who sent the right secret; a deactivated account 403 `service-account-deactivated` (like `user-deactivated`). A failing database is a 5xx, never a refusal. Tokens are accepted only in the `Authorization` header, never in a URL, and no error or log line repeats one.
  - **Failed attempts** (`api/failed-authentications.ts`): each 401 counts against the client's address (Express `req.ip`, the connection's); past `CCMS_AUTH_FAILURES_MAX` (default 20) in `CCMS_AUTH_FAILURES_WINDOW_MS` (default 60 s), a request from it is 429 `too-many-failed-attempts` with `Retry-After` until the window ends: at once with the shared token or none, and with a service token that does not authenticate. A valid service token still acts (its 256 bits cannot be guessed), so a client sharing the address is not locked out. In memory per server, at most 10,000 addresses; behind a reverse proxy every client shares the proxy's address (raise the limit there, or set it to 0). Tests run without the limit unless they set it.
  - **The first token** of a deployment, where nobody can sign in yet: `pnpm nx run api:service-token -- <account> --role <role> [--name <what for>] [--days <1-366>]` (in a deployment `node backend/api/dist/database/service-token.main.js …`) creates the account with those roles when it does not exist, issues a token and prints it once (audited as `service:cli`). Afterwards, use the admin screens.
- **Seed:** `pnpm nx run api:db-seed -- --example` migrates, then writes the example roles (author, contributor (`content.read` and `content.edit` only, #137), reviewer, restricted-type approver, translation manager, source reader, publisher, reader, admin) and eight fictional `sample-*` users holding them (`src/access/repos/example-seed.ts`); `pnpm nx run api:db-seed -- /absolute/path/seed.json` writes your own instead (the same shape, `Seed` in `repos/seed.ts`, a user with an optional `email`; a relative path is read from `backend/api/`). Without either it refuses: the example admin holds every permission, so it is never written by default, and never belongs where people sign in. Idempotent: seeded roles and users are brought to the seed's values and each seeded user holds exactly the seed's roles; roles and users the seed does not name are left alone. A seed may also name `groupMappings` (`[{ "group": "<the provider's group>", "role": "<a seeded role>" }]`, see "Sign-in"): added when missing, never removed; roles a user holds through groups are kept when the seed sets the direct ones. A seeded address that replaces a user's address (ignoring case) ends their links and open code by email, in the seed's transaction (#646, `WaysIn.endMailed`); a seed user without an `email` keeps theirs.

## Sign-in

People sign in to the editor with **OpenID Connect** (#37), at any provider that publishes a discovery document; nothing in the code is tied to one. Off unless `CCMS_OIDC_ISSUER` is set (the settings are in `.env.example`, one block); **demo mode and sign-in cannot be on together** (the settings check refuses to start, as in demo mode anyone with the token acts as anyone). Service accounts keep their own tokens, unchanged.

- **The port** (`core/identity-provider.ts`): `IdentityProvider` (`begin` gives the provider's address and what the callback must check; `complete` turns the callback into an `ExternalIdentity`: issuer, subject, email and whether it is verified, name, username, groups; `signOutUrl`), listed by `IdentityProviders`. OpenID Connect is the core's adapter (`repos/oidc-identity-provider.ts`, `openid-client`): discovery once, lazily, with a 10 s timeout and a 5 s pause after a failure (so the API starts while the provider is down: the sign-in page says "unavailable"); authorization code flow with **PKCE (S256), `state` and `nonce`**; the client authenticates with `client_secret_basic`, or as a public client (PKCE only) without a secret; the userinfo endpoint is read when the ID token lacks the email or the groups. Plain http is accepted only for a loopback issuer (a local test provider). Another adapter (SAML, say) is another `IdentityProvider`; the guard, the sessions and the editor stay as they are.
- **The session policy** (`core/session-policy.ts`, `SessionPolicy`): how long a session lasts (`limits`) and whether a user may sign in with a provider (`maySignIn`); the core's `EnvSessionPolicy` gives everyone the same: **idle 8 hours** (`CCMS_SESSION_IDLE_MINUTES`) and **at most 7 days** after signing in (`CCMS_SESSION_MAX_HOURS`), and lets everyone in. A stricter policy replaces the provider; nothing else changes.
- **The flow** (`api/sign-in.controller.ts`, not JSON:API: addresses a browser is sent to). `GET /auth/<provider>/sign-in?returnTo=<path>` keeps what the callback must check (state, nonce, PKCE verifier, where to go back) in a cookie sealed with the instance secret key (`__Host-openfolia-sign-in`, 10 minutes, HttpOnly, Secure, SameSite=Lax; the key's context names the provider) and answers 303 to the provider. `GET /auth/<provider>/callback` opens that cookie (and clears it: a callback is used once), lets the provider check the answer, and runs `CompleteSignInAction`; then 303 to the editor (`CCMS_EDITOR_URL`, the path `returnTo` named, never another origin or a path outside the editor's). A refusal goes to the editor's `/sign-in?error=<code>`: `provider-unavailable`, `access-denied` (cancelled at the provider), `sign-in-failed` (anything the provider's answer failed: state, nonce, signature; logged without secrets), `sign-in-expired` (no sign-in begun in this browser, or older than 10 minutes), `user-deactivated`, `sign-in-not-allowed` (the policy). `Referrer-Policy: no-referrer` and `Cache-Control: no-store` on both.
- **Who signs in** (`CompleteSignInAction`, one transaction under the access lock): the user linked to that issuer and subject (`user_identities`); else **the user with that email address, only when the provider says it is verified (`email_verified: true`) and the session policy's `linkByEmail` allows it** (`CCMS_OIDC_LINK_BY_EMAIL`): `pending` (the default) only to a user who has never signed in with any provider (one an admin created or the seed wrote, such as the first admin), `any` to any user without an identity at that issuer yet, `off` never; a link is audited `user.identity-link` (`data.issuer`, `data.provider`); else a new user (ID from the username or the email's local part, made unique; audited `user.create` with `data.via: 'sign-in'`). Name and a verified email address follow the provider at each sign-in (`user.update`, `data.via`), an address another user holds excepted. A deactivated user is refused and nothing is kept. **Risk:** linking trusts the provider's `email_verified`. The provider must not let people set an email address it then reports as verified: whoever did would take over the account holding that address here. `pending` limits that to accounts nobody has signed in to yet (made for someone, not yet claimed); `any` reaches every account without an identity at the issuer and is only for providers that guarantee verified addresses; `off` (or seeding no email addresses) avoids linking altogether.
- **Groups and roles** (table `group_roles`; `core/group-mappings.ts`, `api/auth.controller.ts`): the groups claim (`CCMS_OIDC_GROUPS_CLAIM`, default `groups`, or a dotted path such as `org.groups`; at most 200 groups, 256 characters each) is stored with the identity at each sign-in, and a mapping from a group to a role gives the role to every user in the group. `GET /group-mappings` (paginated, `filter[group]`, `filter[role]`), `GET /group-mappings/:mappingId`, `POST /group-mappings` (`attributes.group`, `relationships.role`; 409 `group-mapping-exists`, 422 `unknown-role`) and `DELETE /group-mappings/:mappingId`, all `admin.roles`, all applied at once to the users already in the group (`role.assign` / `role.unassign` with `data.via: 'groups'`), audited as `group-mapping.create` / `.delete`. Removing a mapping that would leave no admin is 409 `last-admin`. A role a mapping gives cannot be deleted (409 `role-in-use`, `meta.groupMappings`). In `role_assignments` a role is held `direct` (given by an admin, as before), `via_groups`, or both: taking a direct role leaves one the groups give, a group's going leaves one given directly. `GET /users/:userId` says which are the groups' (`meta.groupRoleIds`), and `PATCH /users/:userId` with `relationships.roles` sets the direct ones only. **At sign-in the provider's groups apply as they are**, also when that takes the last admin's role (the provider is the authority on its groups; give the first admin their role directly to keep it).
- **Sessions** (`sessions`; `core/sessions.ts`, `SessionStore`): an opaque token (`ccms_ses_` and 64 hex digits from the CSPRNG) in the cookie `__Host-openfolia-session` (HttpOnly, Secure, SameSite=Lax, Path=/, the longest lifetime as its Max-Age); only its SHA-256 is stored. Signing in again ends the browser's previous session; a user keeps at most 20, the oldest end first. The cookie name and the token's prefix are each one constant (`api/session-cookies.ts`, `core/sessions.ts`).
- **Every request** (`ActorGuard`): with sign-in on (a provider, passwords or codes by email: `SignInSettings.signInOn`, #639), a session cookie is checked first, and alone decides who acts (`AuthenticateSessionAction`: a session past its idle time or its longest lifetime is 401 `session-expired`, an unknown or signed-out one 401 `unauthorized`, a deactivated user 403 `user-deactivated`; never counted as a failed authentication); the `Authorization` header beside it is ignored. On routes anyone may read (`@OptionalActor()`, the stable addresses `/d/…`) a valid session acts as its person, so a `signed-in` address (and its QR code) opens in the editor, but a `public` address reads no credentials at all and answers as the public (#608); a session that cannot act (ended, unknown, deactivated) is ignored there, never a 401 nor a failure counted, so the request reads as without credentials. The editor's nginx drops the cookie on its root `/d/`, so a scanned QR code always reads as anyone; its `/api/d/` keeps it. **The shared token cannot be set with sign-in** (`CCMS_API_TOKEN`; the settings check refuses it, but for a loopback issuer in local runs and tests): it reads every ref without signing in, so an editor proxy adding it would open drafts to anyone; machine clients use service account tokens. The actor is read from the database on every request, uncached: **a role change, a mapping change or a deactivation applies from the next request**. `X-Ccms-User` with a session is 400 `user-header-not-accepted`. A cookie-authenticated `POST`, `PUT`, `PATCH` or `DELETE` must come from the editor's origin (`Origin`, `CCMS_EDITOR_URL` and `CCMS_EDITOR_ORIGINS`), else 403 `cross-origin-request`; with SameSite=Lax and the JSON:API media type rule that closes cross-site requests twice. Responses vary by `Cookie` (on `/d/…` only those a session acted on, never a `public` address's, so a shared cache keeps one copy of it). A session's last use is kept to the minute.
- **The editor's routes**: `GET /auth/providers` (public, paginated: id, the button's `name` from `CCMS_OIDC_NAME`, `links.signIn`); `POST /auth/sign-out` (public; ends the cookie's session, audited `user.sign-out`, clears the cookie and answers `meta.signOutUrl`, the provider's end-session address with `post_logout_redirect_uri` = the editor's `/sign-in`, or null); `GET /me` has `meta.session` (`provider`, `expiresAt`) for a signed-in session. Signing in is audited `user.sign-in` (`data.provider`, `created`, `linked`).
- **Setting it up**: register a client at the provider (authorization code, PKCE) with the redirect URL `<editor>/api/auth/oidc/callback` and the post-logout redirect URL `<editor>/sign-in`; set `CCMS_OIDC_ISSUER`, `CCMS_OIDC_CLIENT_ID`, `CCMS_OIDC_REDIRECT_URL` (and the secret, for a confidential client); seed the first admin with their verified email address (or a `groupMappings` entry for the admins' group) before the first sign-in. The instance secret key ("Secrets") must be available: it seals the sign-in cookie.
- **Email and password** (#610; `CCMS_PASSWORD_SIGN_IN`, on by default, off in demo mode, where `on` is refused at start-up): beside the providers, into the same sessions (`provider` `password`), cookie, sign-out and audit. Tables `user_passwords` (one argon2id hash per user) and `sign_in_links` (one open link per user: only the SHA-256 of its token, `ccms_pwl_` and 64 hex digits; migration `0054_passwords`).
  - **Hashing** (`repos/argon2-password-hasher.ts`, port `PasswordHasher`): argon2id from Node's own `crypto.argon2`, in the PHC string format, its parameters in one place (`ARGON2_PARAMETERS`: 19 MiB, 2 passes, 1 lane); a hash made with other parameters is replaced at its user's next sign-in. At most 2 hashes at once and 64 waiting, past that 503 `sign-in-busy`. Passwords are compared in their NFC form.
  - **The rules** (`core/passwords.ts`, `passwordProblem`): 12 to 256 characters, not a common password (a bundled list, its look-alike spellings, keyboard runs, a repeated pattern) and not the person's own address; no composition rules. 422 `password-too-short`, `password-too-long`, `password-too-common`.
  - **Signing in** (`POST /auth/password/sign-in`, public, from the editor's origin): an unknown address, a person without a password and a wrong password are all 401 `invalid-credentials`, after the same work (a dummy hash is checked); a deactivated user or the policy is said only with the right password. Each attempt counts against the client's address (`FailedAuthentications`, `CCMS_AUTH_FAILURES_*`) and toward the account's sign-ins (`PasswordAttempts`, `CCMS_PASSWORD_FAILURES_*`, 10 per 15 minutes by default, on even when the address limit is off), counted in the same database statement as the limit's test, before the check, and taken back when right; past either limit, 429 before any password is checked. "Forgot password" requests for the address count apart under the same limit, so failed sign-ins never block a reset nor resets a sign-in; changing one's password counts under the user. The counts live in `password_attempts`, one row per key, the key only as an HMAC (so no address is stored, a known and an unknown one are kept alike, and nothing evicts a running window) under a key derived from the instance secret key (HKDF), else made once and kept in `password_attempts_key`: shared by every server and kept across restarts. Ended windows are deleted. Turning `CCMS_PASSWORD_SIGN_IN` off stops the password sessions (they act again if it is turned back on before they end). Audited `user.sign-in` (`data.method` `password`, `rehashed`).
  - **Invites and reset links** (`POST /users/:userId/invite`, `POST /users/:userId/password-reset`, `DELETE /users/:userId/invite`, `admin.roles`): a one-time link `<editor>/set-password#<token>` (the token after `#`, so no server logs it), shown once, emailed too when email is set up (templates `invite`, `password-reset`); a new link replaces the person's earlier one. An invite works `CCMS_INVITE_DAYS` (7) days, a reset link 1 hour. `POST /auth/password/link` says what a link is for; `POST /auth/password/set` sets the password, uses the link, **ends every session of the person** and signs this browser in. Audited `user.invite`, `user.password-reset-requested` (`data.via` `admin` or `email`; the first admin's link from the command line is a `user.invite` with `via` `cli`), `user.invite-withdraw`, `user.password-set`: IDs only, never a token, a link or a hash.
  - **Forgot password** (`POST /auth/password/forgot`, offered only with email and `CCMS_PUBLIC_URL`, else 404 `not-configured`): always 202 with the same answer, at once; the work runs after the answer, at most one reset email a minute per person, and only for a person who has a password (an invited or provider-only person gets nothing: an admin's invite gives a password, an email alone does not). Its requests count under a budget of their own per address.
  - **Changing one's password** (`POST /me/password`): the current password (a wrong one is 403 `wrong-password` and counts as a failure), the rules, then the other sessions end; this one goes on. Audited `user.password-changed`.
  - **The first admin**: `pnpm nx run api:first-admin -- <email> [--name <name>] [--editor <url>]` (`node dist/database/first-admin.main.js`) makes the user and the role `admin` when missing and prints an invite link; `api:setup-local` runs it into a fresh local database. **From the environment** (#373, `repos/first-admin-at-start.ts`): with `CCMS_FIRST_ADMIN_EMAIL` (and `CCMS_FIRST_ADMIN_NAME`) set, the API's start (`api` and `all`, never a worker) does the same, but only into an instance without any user, checked under the access lock in the same transaction (two servers starting at once make one admin), and logs the link, valid one hour (`FIRST_ADMIN_LINK_MS`; none with password sign-in off, where they sign in with the provider, linked by their verified address, or (#639) by a code by email at that address, which the log says, nor with `CCMS_FIRST_ADMIN_LINK_LOG=false`, where `first-admin` prints one on the terminal); a first provider sign-in that links a user by email, and every sign-in by email code, closes that user's open invite (`user.invite-withdraw`, `data.via` `sign-in`), the start-up's link included; on an instance with a user it changes nothing and logs that the setting is ignored, so it works once. Audited as `service:startup`, the invite with `via` `environment`. A role `admin` without `admin.roles` stops the start. `GET /auth/options` tells the sign-in page what to show; `GET /users` has `meta.signIn` (`password`, `provider`, `inviteExpiresAt`).
- **A one-time code by email** (#634; `CCMS_EMAIL_CODE_SIGN_IN`, on by default wherever email is set up and password sign-in is on, beside a provider alone, or alone without passwords or a provider (#639), only when set to `on`, which needs `CCMS_MAIL_URL`; never in demo mode; `GET /auth/options` `emailCode`): into the same sessions (`provider` `email-code`), cookie, sign-out and audit. Table `email_sign_in_codes` (migration `0055_email_codes`): one open code per user, apart from `sign_in_links` (a code never replaces an invite); `email_code_sign_ins` records who has signed in with a code, so `CCMS_OIDC_LINK_BY_EMAIL=pending` treats them like someone with a password. The code only as an HMAC-SHA256 of the user and the code under a key derived (HKDF) from the instance secret key, which email needs anyway (port `EmailCodeHasher`); the link's token (`ccms_ecl_` and 64 hex digits) only as its SHA-256.
  - **Asking** (`POST /auth/email-code/request`, public, from the editor's origin): always 202 with the same answer, at once; the work runs after it. For an active person with the address (with a password or without one), a six-digit code and a link `<public URL>/sign-in-code#<token>`, valid `CCMS_EMAIL_CODE_MINUTES` (10) minutes, replace their earlier code, and the email (template `email-code`, in the sign-in page's `language`, else `CCMS_MAIL_LANGUAGE`) is queued in the same transaction. Each request counts against the client's address (`code-request:`) and toward the address's own budget (`PasswordAttempts` key `code-request:`, `CCMS_PASSWORD_FAILURES_*`). Audited `user.email-code-requested` by `service:sign-in`.
  - **Signing in** (`POST /auth/email-code/sign-in`): an unknown address, no code, a wrong code, a used one and one past its time are all 401 `invalid-code`, after the same work (one lookup, one HMAC, a constant-time comparison); a deactivated user or the policy is said only with the right code. Each try counts against the client's address (as a failed authentication) and toward the address's `code-attempt:` budget, before the check, taken back when right. The try that uses up the budget revokes the code when wrong; tries past it are 429, compare nothing and leave a newer code in place for after the window. Each code also takes five tries at most, counted before the comparison in one statement (`EmailCodes.claim`), however many come at once. The budgets `code-request:` and `code-attempt:` are apart from each other and from `sign-in:` and `reset:`. The code is removed in the transaction that starts the session. Audited `user.sign-in` (`data.method` `email-code`, `via` `code`).
  - **The link** (`POST /auth/email-code/link`, the token in the body): signs in and uses the code with it; 404 `link-invalid`, 410 `link-expired`. Audited `user.sign-in` (`via` `link`). Turning `CCMS_EMAIL_CODE_SIGN_IN` off refuses all three (409 `email-code-sign-in-off`) and ends the sessions begun with a code (`EnvSessionPolicy`).
  - **What ends an open code** (#645, `core/ways-in.ts`, `WaysIn`, in the transaction of the change): setting a password through a link, changing one's password and deactivating the account end every way in (the sessions, but for a password change the request's own; the links to set a password; the open code and its link); a new address (or none) in place of one, from an admin (`PATCH /users/:userId`) or from the provider at sign-in, ends what was emailed to the old one (the links and the code; sessions stay; a first address ends nothing, so a link copied for someone without one keeps working). The seed (`db-seed`) does the same when it gives a seeded user another address (#646). A code ended so answers like a wrong one (401 `invalid-code`; its link 404 `link-invalid`).
