- **Merge** checks the approvals again (409 `not-approved`, `meta.pending`; `approval-lapsed`, above), against the base commit each attempt merges onto, so a type restricted by a push to the base between two attempts is checked too, then refuses a base rewound past the change set's base commit (409 `base-rewritten`: the merge would bring in what nobody reviewed) and merges exactly the review head into the base branch with `ChangeSetBranches.merge` (compare-and-swap on the base's head, tried again on top of a base that moved, at most 3 times, then 409 `base-moved`), always a merge commit with one `Approved-by` per approver. Then `merged` with `mergeCommit`, `change-set.merge` (`data: { commit, into, fastForward, approvedBy }`; outbox key: the change set's ID, so it is recorded once), the locks released and any working copies dropped. The answer has `meta.merge: { commit, fastForward }`. **A conflict** is answered as data: 409 `merge-conflict` with `meta.conflicts` (`{ path, kinds }`, at most 100) and `meta.total`; nothing is written and the change set stays approved but unmerged. A merge that would leave content invalid under the configuration it keeps is 422 `config-in-use`, the same (#253, "Configuration usage check").
- **Timeouts.** Every transition's transaction is bounded (`BOUNDED`, as save points and abandoning are): past the write lock or statement timeout it rolls back and answers 503 `database-timeout` with `Retry-After`. A merge that landed in Git before a timeout is recorded by the next return, abandon or reconciler pass (below).
- **Git first, then the database.** The merge is made inside the transaction and recorded after. Return and abandon of an approved change set first ask whether its review head is in its base already (a merge whose recording failed): it is then recorded as merged (`Workflow.landedMerge`) and they answer 409 `invalid-state` with `meta.state: "merged"`, so a merge is never lost from the database and the audit log. If the transaction fails, or the process stops, after the merge landed, the change set is still `approved` with its review head in its base's history: `ReconcileChangeSetMergesAction` (run by `ChangeSetReconciler` after the commits pass; it walks the approved change sets oldest first in keyset pages of 100, at most 5 pages and within the pass's time budget, `RUN_BUDGET_MS`, 2 minutes, shared with the branch and commit passes, which defer what is left; the next pass resumes after the last one it looked at, and one that reached the end starts at the oldest again; the place is kept per process) records it then, from reachability, not trailers (`data.recovered: true`, as `service:reconciler`: a commit's trailers are only what it says of itself, so the merger its `Merged-by` trailer names is kept as `data.claimedMergedBy`, never as the actor); a merge tried again finds it up to date and records it too, as long as its approvals still hold (else it answers 409 `approval-lapsed`, and the return that follows records the merge).

- **Self-approval** (#588; the installation setting `selfApproval`, `GET`/`PATCH /settings`, see "Installation settings"): `never` (the default: four eyes, as above), `review` (the author may give the review approval of their own change set; a restricted type's sign-off still needs someone else: their own is 403) or `review-and-sign-off` (they may give both, taking it to `approved` alone). Read through the core port `SelfApprovalRule` (`core/self-approval.ts`) in the approval's own transaction, when it is given (`Workflow.ownApproval`); scope and permissions are checked as for anyone, so the author still needs `content.review` (and `content.approve` for a sign-off) over everything it changes. An author never returns their own change set, whatever the rule. A self-approval is recorded with `self: true` (column `change_set_approvals.self`, migration `0049_self_approval`), its `change-set.approve` or `change-set.sign-off` audit event and outbox row carry `self: true`, and `meta.review` says so (`approvals[].self`, `selfApproved`; `selfApproval` is the rule now, for the review screen). Approvals given stay when the rule changes back to `never`: as for role changes (#180), merge re-checks only each approver's role and scope (`ApprovalStanding`), never the rule, so a self-approval lapses like any other when its author loses the role.
- **Who counts as its author** (#597; `ChangeSetAuthors`, `core/change-set-authors.ts`, read through `Workflow.authorsOf`): everyone who wrote content in it, not only the recorded `author`. Worked out on the server when an approval, sign-off or return is given (and for `meta.review.yours`), never taken from the client, from two sources:
  - **Its commits:** the recorded author plus the writer of every commit of the change set's own line, from its base commit to the head approved or returned (its review head, else its branch's head): `ChangeSetBranches.writers`, the first-parent history read from Git, leaving out the base commit's history (a refresh moves the base commit on, so the base's own commits never count).
  - **Its links** (kept in Postgres, so no push can rewrite them): followed back through every carry-forward (`change_set_carry_forwards`, #60) and move onto a rewritten base (`change_set_supersessions`, #471), every author of the change set it carries or was moved from, at that change set's branch head (its review head once the branch is gone), up to 50 links. So the writer of a fix carried forward by someone else stays its author, also when nothing applied and the carrier typed it again by hand.
  - **How a commit maps to a user:** the identity the API writes into its own commits (`gitIdentity`, `repos/git-change-set-branches.ts`: the actor's ID as the name, `<id>@<kind>.openfolia.invalid` as the address, and the trailer `Actor: <kind>/<id>`). A commit counts when its last `Actor` trailer names an actor whose identity is its Git author's address, as for `Merged-by` (`mergedInto`). It then also names each actor in its **`Carried-author: <kind>/<id>`** trailers, which a carry-forward or a move writes for the authors of the change set it carries (leaving out the carrier, the commit's actor), so the history says who wrote it too. The message cannot forge a trailer (`invalid-message`).
  - **Commits made outside the API** (a push, an import with a Git identity of its own) and updates from the base (merge commits with `Updated-by`, no `Actor`) name nobody: an unmapped author does not count. Only first parents are read, so a commit brought in by a pushed merge does not either. A push can make a user count by claiming the API's identity for them, or drop a commit's `Carried-author` trailers by rewriting the branch (a moved head returns the change set to draft first); the links above do not depend on Git, so a carried writer stays counted either way. Being counted only ever restricts: every right an author has (reading their own work, abandoning, moving it) still goes by the recorded author.
  - A line longer than `MAX_WRITER_COMMITS` (20,000 commits) cannot be read whole: approve, sign-off, return, the review read, carry-forward and rebase answer 409 **`too-many-commits`** (`meta.max`) rather than guess. The authors are read before the transition's transaction, never while its row is held, and only for a change set the actor may see in a state the transition takes: to anyone else it stays 404 (or 409 `invalid-state`) exactly as before, and a failure reading them is told only once the change set is found for them.
  - `meta.review.yours` (on `GET /change-sets/:changeSetId/review` and the answers to approve and sign-off) says whether the reader counts as an author; who else counts is not told. The review screen blocks Approve, Sign off and Return on it as for the recorded author.

## Updating from the base

A draft whose base branch moved on since it started (#22) is brought up to date by its author, never automatically (`oq.workflow`: a merge that conflicts is resolved by its author updating from `main` and fixing the files in the editor).

- **`meta.behind`** on `GET /change-sets/:changeSetId` (`ChangeSetBehind`): for an open change set, how many commits the base branch's head has that its branch does not (`git rev-list --count`, counted up to 1000; absent when the branch or the base is gone, or the base is a draft the caller may not read); null when the base was rewritten, below).
- **`meta.base.state`** beside it: `current`, or `rewritten` (#471) when the base branch's history no longer holds the change set's base commit (its head is not that commit and has not it as an ancestor: a force-push or a restore, `baseRewritten`). Such a change set can be neither updated (`refresh`: 409 `base-rewritten`) nor merged (409 `base-rewritten`): what it changes is counted from that commit, so either would bring in what nobody reviewed.
- **Moving it onto the current base** (#471): **`POST /change-sets/:changeSetId/rebase`** (`RebaseChangeSetAction`; `content.edit`, its author only, since it closes the change set: 403 for anyone else; 404 for a change set the actor may not see), for an open change set whose base was rewritten (409 `base-not-rewritten` otherwise: update it instead; `already-superseded` with `meta.changeSetId` for one moved already, also once closed; `invalid-state` for another one no longer open; `base-missing`, `branch-missing`). The carry is planned before anything is written (422 `too-many-changes` leaves the change set and its locks as they were). A new draft change set on the base's current head, its author's, with the same title and description, holding the change set's own diff (its base commit to its branch's head) applied three-way, exactly as carrying a fix forward applies one (`CarryChange`, `core/carry-change.ts`, shared with `CarryForwardChangeSetAction`): its subjects locked to the new change set, what applied committed as one save point, the rest left as the base has it and listed in `meta.rebase.conflicts` with the same kinds and `blockIds` (`meta.rebase`: `from`, `commit`, `applied`, `conflicts`, `total`). Working copies are committed first as a save point for a draft (`CommitWorkingCopiesAction`); a change set in review is 409 `uncommitted-working-copies` (`meta.subjects`) while it has some. Approvals do not carry over. **Order:** the new branch (Git), then one transaction holding the old change set's row that checks nothing supersedes it yet (409 `already-superseded`, `meta.changeSetId`), records the new change set (`change-set.create`, `data.supersedes`) and the link (`change_set_supersessions`, migration `0032_change_set_supersessions`: `change_set_id` the new one, `superseded_id` the old one, both → `change_sets`, never updated), and releases the old one's locks (`lock.release`, `data.reason: "change-set.rebase"`); then the locks and the save point, whose transaction abandons the old one (`change-set.abandon` with `data.supersededBy`, its outbox row too) and records `change-set.rebase` on it (`data: { changeSetId, baseRef, onto, commit?, applied, conflicts }`). A request cut off before the save point leaves the old one open and the new one an empty draft: the same request again goes on with that draft (or, when its commit landed, only closes the old one). `GET /change-sets/:changeSetId` links the two in `meta.supersededBy` (the newest) and `meta.supersedes`; the old one's branch, comments and history stay readable.
- **`POST /change-sets/:changeSetId/refresh`** (`RefreshChangeSetAction`; `content.edit`, its author only: 403 for anyone else who may read drafts, 404 for one who may not; a draft only, 409 `invalid-state`): merges the base's head, read as the author may read it (a draft base they may not read is 409 `base-missing`, as a gone one), into the branch (`ChangeSetBranches.refresh`: `GitWriter.merge` onto the change-set branch, compare-and-swap on its head). A branch with no commits of its own is fast-forwarded; otherwise a merge commit with parents (branch head, base head), the author as its author and the trailers `Updated-change-set` and `Updated-by` (never a save point's or a merge's). Its `baseCommit` moves on to the base's head (`baseReleased` stays true only while that head is released), so what it changes, its review and taking changes out (`reverts`) are counted from there. A base rewound past its base commit is 409 `base-rewritten`. Body: none, or `{ meta: { comment?, keep?, keepMine? } }`. The answer has `meta.refresh: { updated, head, fastForward, savePoint }`; `updated: false` when the branch held the base's head already (nothing written).
  - **Conflicts write nothing**: 409 `refresh-conflict` with `meta.conflicts` (`{ path, subject?, kinds }`, at most 100) and `meta.total`. Besides git's kinds (`contents`, `modify/delete`, …), `working-copy` names a working copy not yet committed of a file the base changed: commit or discard it first, or keep it.
  - **Reported** (#437): each module, manual, configuration or term an answer lists (at most 100) is recorded in `refresh_conflicts` (`RefreshConflictStore`; migration `0031_refresh_conflicts`) with the base head it was reported against, anew where none was or where the base's file changed since the last report (`changedPaths` between the two base heads; one that cannot be told is reported anew). A working-copy save (`SaveWorkingCopyAction`, in its transaction) sets the report's `saved_at`, only when that transaction began after the report (a save begun before it was not made seeing it); discarding the working copy (`DiscardWorkingCopyAction`) sets it back to null, since what was saved is gone. The editor also waits for its own saves on their way before it updates. `GET /change-sets/:changeSetId/refresh-conflicts` (its author only, paged) lists them with `savedAt`: what the editor's list marks as saved. Cleared when an update goes through (or finds the branch up to date).
  - **Resolving**: the author makes the conflicting files what they should be in the editor (taking the base's change into their own), saves them, and updates again with them in `meta.keep`; or keeps a file as it is by explicit choice with `meta.keepMine` ("Keep my version": the base's change to it is dropped where it conflicts). At most 100 subjects in both; one in both is 422 `duplicate-subject`. A subject in `meta.keep` whose keeping matters (its file conflicts, or its working copy is in the update's way and is committed first) is taken only once it was saved since its report, against a base whose file is still the one reported: otherwise 409 `refresh-unresolved` (`meta.unresolved`: `{ path, subject }`, `meta.total`), nothing is written (no save point, no merge), and a subject with no report, or whose file the base changed since, is reported now, so a save from then on counts. A save only counts after the report: one made before it, or a working copy committed unchanged, does not. Saves made through the API on the author's behalf (an import, a translation import) count as saves. Their working copies are committed first, as a save point (`CommitWorkingCopiesAction`: every check a save point makes; `meta.refresh.savePoint`), then the merge keeps the branch's file where it conflicts (`MergeRequest.keep` in `content-repo`: a plain file conflict only, `contents`, `add/add` or `modify/delete`; any other stays a conflict). A kept subject that conflicts needs the change set's lock (409 `lock-required`) and the author's scope over it as committed (403).
  - **Recorded** in one bounded transaction holding the change set's row and the kept subjects' locks: a save point left unrecorded below the head first (`CommitRecorder.catchUp`), then the Git merge, then the new base commit, an outbox row for the merge commit (topic `change-set.commit`, as a save point's, so recovery's walk back stops there; its `written` and `deleted` are what the base brought in), working copies whose file did not change moved on to the new head (one version further, so an open tab reads them again) and one `change-set.refresh` audit event (`data: { baseRef, from, to, parent, commit, fastForward, written, deleted, kept, resolvedByEditing, keptByChoice, workingCopiesMoved, savePoint? }`: `kept` the subjects whose conflict kept the branch's file, `resolvedByEditing` and `keptByChoice` the kept subjects whose keeping mattered, by how they were resolved). A branch that moved meanwhile is planned again, at most 3 times (then 409 `branch-moved`). **Git first**: if the transaction fails after the merge landed, the change set keeps its old base commit until an update is tried again, which finds the branch up to date and records it then (`data.recovered: true`).
  - Content left invalid by what the base took away (a configuration value removed there and used here) is not refused: the author sees it in the editor and fixes it; merging refuses it (`config-in-use`) until then.

## Changes and status

Derived from Git on every request, never stored (#23).

- **`GET /change-sets/:changeSetId/changes`** (`content.read`; the change set found as `GET /change-sets/:changeSetId` finds it, so another author's is 404 for an actor without `content.read.draft`): the modules and manuals that differ between the change set's base commit and its head: the review head once submitted (what is reviewed and merged), else its branch's head. `ListChangeSetChangesAction` takes the changed files from Git as review does (`changedPathsWithin`, `core/review-scope.ts`: 422 `too-many-changes` past `MAX_REVIEWED_PATHS`; a branch that is gone is 409 `branch-missing`) and reads each side through `ReadableContent`: the base by its commit ID (for the change set's author, when it started from released content, as released content however far `main` has moved on: `OpenSnapshotAction`'s `authoredBase`, given only after `opensAsAuthor`; their branch holds all of it but what they changed), the head by the branch's name where it is the branch's head (an author opens their own branch, #126; a submitted change set whose branch moved past its review head opens that by commit ID, so for an author without `content.read.draft` it is then 404). A module or manual the reader may not see on a side is not there on that side, exactly like a missing one; one they see on neither is not listed and not counted (filtered before paging), so neither the list nor `meta.page.total` says what lies outside their scope. The configuration (`config`) and terms (`term/<termId>`) are listed too, for every reader of the change set (they have no audience); other files (templates, images) are not. Each commit opens with the permission it takes (a released base with `content.read`, a draft head with `content.read.draft`), so each side shows only what the reader would see on the other too (`seenOnBoth`, `core/diff-sides.ts`, over `ReadableContent.wouldSee`): a reader whose draft scope is narrower than their released one does not learn of a draft change outside it from the released side. A manifest names on each side only the modules it may name to the reader on both (`ReadableContent.namedHere`; a module with a file on one side only is judged there under both permissions), so its `entries` differ only where the content does.
  - A `ChangeSetChange` (`id` `module/<lang>/<moduleId>`, `manual/<manualId>`, `config` or `term/<termId>`) has `attributes.subject` (as locks name it), `relationships.changeSet` and, all derived, `meta.change` (`added`, `changed`, `removed`), `meta.fields` (the module's metadata fields, the manifest's members, the configuration's members, an item of a list with IDs as `<member>/<id>` such as `variantAttributes/model` and the member alone when only the order changed, or the term's members, a language as `languages/<lang>`, that differ), `meta.blocks` (`{ blockId, change }`) and `meta.unreadable` (a side's file cannot be read; reported only to a reader who may learn of broken files). The document's `meta` also names the two commits (`base`, `head`). Sorted by subject (the configuration, manuals, modules by language and ID, then terms), paged.
  - **Blocks by ID** (`diffBlocks`, `core/change-set-diff.ts`): every block, list item and procedure step is compared by its ID. A container's own content holds the IDs of what is inside it, in order, but not their content: an edit in a section's paragraph is the paragraph's alone, while adding, removing or reordering what a container holds changes the container too. A table's own content is its layout (rows and cells, with the IDs of each cell's blocks), so a row added above leaves the blocks below unchanged and a block moved between cells changes the table. A node is `changed` when its own content or its parent differs. An unchanged block keeps its ID and is never listed; a module whose content is the same on both sides is not listed at all.
  - Diffs are kept per pair of commits and what the reader sees on each (`readingKey`): the last `DIFFS_KEPT` (32), least recently used dropped (`Recent`, `base/core/recent.ts`).
- **Module status**, `meta.status` on `GET /modules` and `GET /modules/:lang/:id` (`GetModuleStatusesAction`, `modules/core/`), for the module as read at `?ref=`:
  - `release`: the newest release tag holding the module as the reader may see it there (every tag counts as a release, as for reading: `released.ts`; newest by when the tag was made, `ContentRepository.releases`; at most the 50 newest are looked through; each tag's module listing is read once, `GitRepo.fileIds`), with its commit and whether the module's file there is exactly the one read (`same`, by Git blob: `moduleRevision`); `null` when none holds it. A release where it lies outside the reader's scope does not count, so an older one may. Kept per set of releases, reading key and module (the last 10,000).
  - `main`: the module on `main` as the reader may see it there, with `same`; `null` when it is not there.
  - `changeSets`: the open change sets (`draft`, `in-review`, `approved`) whose base-to-head diff changes the module's file (`ChangeSetTouches`, `change-sets/core/`; the newest 500 looked at, changed paths kept per pair of commits), newest first. Only change sets the reader may read (every one with `content.read.draft`, else their own), and only where the change set's diff would list the module for them (seen on its head or base, through `seenOnBoth`). Counted by the files that differ, so a change only in formatting (the same parsed module) names the change set though its diff lists nothing; a change set past `MAX_REVIEWED_PATHS` is named for none. Left out entirely for a reader holding neither `content.read.draft` nor `content.edit`.
  - `deprecated`: the module's deprecation at the ref read (#266), `{ reason, replacedBy? }` or `null`: its source-language file's as the reader may see it there, else (none they see, or it cannot be read) the file's own, so every language of a module says the same (`deprecationIn`). `attributes.deprecated` is the file's own, as stored. Read on `main`, it says what was merged; read on a change set's branch, what that change set would merge.
  - **Caching:** a status depends on more than the commit read (tags, `main`, open change sets, whose they are), so the module routes are `@BeyondTheCommit()` (`content/api/read-content.ts`): `private, no-cache` also for a commit ID, and the ETag is a hash of the answer, checked after it is made, so a 304 never stands for a status that changed.
  - **Delivery API:** the module routes are part of it, so `ModuleStatus` is in `openapi/delivery.yaml`: `release` and `main` are released content, which a consumer may read anyway. `changeSets` is never there for a consumer's token (`content.read` alone): consumers learn nothing of drafts, not even that there are none. `GET /change-sets/:changeSetId/changes` is not a delivery route.

## Deprecation

A module or manual withdrawn or superseded (#266) is deprecated by a content change, as any edit: its metadata (`meta.deprecated` in the source-language module file, `deprecated` in a manifest; the content model's `Deprecation`, `{ reason, replacedBy? }`) changed in a change set, with its locks, working copy, review and merge. No route of its own and no extra sign-off: the change set's normal review decides (the default while #266's open question is unanswered).

- **Deprecated content stays readable and stays in manuals** until someone removes it; nothing blocks. Where it is still used is a warning: `deprecated-module` on each manual's checks that includes it (directly or through module references; `params.module`, and `params.replacedBy` when it names one), and `unknown-replacement` where a deprecation names a module or manual that does not exist (told only to a reader who learns of missing modules, as `missing-module`; `ReadableContent.missingManual` for manuals) or itself (the content model's `validateModule`/`validateManifest`).
- **Reads:** `GET /modules` and `GET /modules/:lang/:id`: `attributes.deprecated` (the file's own) and `meta.status.deprecated` (the module's: its source-language file's); `GET /module-summaries`: `attributes.deprecated` (the module's) and `filter[deprecated]=true|false`; `GET /manuals` and `GET /manuals/:id`: `attributes.deprecated`. All of them are delivery routes, so a consumer sees it (`openapi/delivery.yaml`).
- **Module index** (#45): each module file and manifest keeps its `deprecated` (migration `0028_module_index_deprecated`, which drops the commits indexed before so they are indexed again), and `meta.usedIn` says of each manual and module using a module whether it is deprecated (`ModuleUse.deprecated`), so an author sees which uses still count.
- **Outbox** (`DeprecationEvents`, `change-sets/core/deprecation-events.ts`): recording a merge into `main` (`Workflow.recordMerge`, a recovered merge too; a maintenance line's merge writes none, its change is written when carried forward) writes one `module.deprecated` row per module whose source-language file is deprecated at the merged review head and was not at the change set's base commit (a new module added deprecated counts too), and one `manual.deprecated` per manifest alike: `{ moduleId, lang (the source language) | manualId, reason, replacedBy (null for none), changeSetId, commit }` (the merge commit), keyed `<changeSetId>/<id>` so it is written once. Read from Git unfiltered, whatever the audience, for consumers that decide who is told (webhooks #42, the change feed #43, notifications #252): one that passes a row on applies its readers' rules to it first; a changed reason or an undone deprecation writes nothing. Git failing while the rows are worked out is logged and writes none: the merge is recorded all the same.

## Module index

The `modules` component's index (#45, FR-19): per commit, every module file with the metadata lists filter and sort by (title, type, audience, facets, keywords) the modules its reference blocks name and the variant attributes and values its variant blocks name, and every manifest with its title, audience, the modules its entries name and the variant attributes and values it names (its combinations and text attributes' conditions in `variants`; each entry's condition apart, in `entry_variants`, with the modules the entry covers, so a reader is told it only where they may see one of them; `core/variant-names.ts`, #91, migration `0021_module_index_variants`, which drops the commits indexed before so they are indexed again on their next read, its columns defaulting to empty so a server still on the previous version keeps writing during a rolling deploy). `ModuleIndex` (`core/module-index.ts`) builds a commit's index from Git when the commit is first read, so every change (a save point, a merge, a release, a push) is indexed on the next read of it; it is kept in Postgres (`ModuleIndexStore`, `DrizzleModuleIndexStore` over `module_index_commits`, `module_index_modules` and `module_index_manuals`, migration `0018_module_index`; the newest 64 commits, older ones built again when asked for) and in memory (16 commits). A commit never changes, so neither does its index; two servers building the same commit keep the first.

- **Rebuildable:** derived from Git alone, never edited. `pnpm nx run api:index-rebuild` (in a deployment `node backend/api/dist/modules/index-rebuild.main.js`; reads `CCMS_CONTENT_DIR` and `CCMS_DATABASE_URL`, applies pending migrations first) drops every kept index and indexes the commit of every branch and tag again; any other commit is indexed on its first read. Dropping the tables by hand is just as safe: the next read builds them again, with the same result (`tests/module-index.e2e.spec.ts`).
- **Scope:** the index holds the files unfiltered; who may see what is decided when it is read, never stored. `ReadableModuleIndex` (`core/readable-module-index.ts`) keeps only what the reader may see, judged by `ReadableContent.judgeIndexed` exactly as `modules()` and `manuals()` judge the files (a module by its metadata, a broken file only for a reader of everything in its language, a manual by its audience), per commit and reading key (the last 64). A use is named and counted only when the reader may see the manual or module using it, and a manual counts through module references only where the reader may see every module on the way, so neither `meta.usedIn` nor `meta.usage` says anything about what lies outside the scope. The page's files are still read through `ReadableContent`, which checks the scope again.
- **`GET /modules`:** `filter[lang]`, the module filters shared with `GET /module-summaries` (`filter[type]`, `filter[audience]`, `filter[facet]`, `filter[keyword]`), `filter[product]` (a value of the product facet: `release.productFacet`, else the first facet, as releases name products; organisation-agnostic, so no facet name is built in), `filter[status]` (`released`: the newest release holding the module holds exactly this file; `changed`: a release holds it, this file differs; `unreleased`: none of the 50 newest does; `in-change-set`: an open change set the reader may read changes it, never for a reader who is not told of change sets) and `sort` (`id`, `lang`, `title`, `type`, `usage`, comma-separated, `-` for descending, each once; ties by language, then ID; default `lang,id`; an unreadable file sorts after the rest by title and type). Filters and the sort run on the index before paging, so `meta.page.total` counts what is listed; `filter[status]` works out the status of every module left by the other filters (cached as for the status itself), the others read only the index. A filter on metadata or status leaves unreadable files out. A value the configuration does not know matches nothing; a form that is not a configuration key, an unknown status or sort field is 400 `invalid-parameter`.
- **Lists paged on it** (#480): each module file also keeps a `digest` (SHA-256 of the parsed module's JSON: equal digests, equal modules, whatever the formatting), a source-language file its translatable units (`units`, by block ID in document order) and a translation with a readable record that record compared with the source file at the same commit (`translation`: the units outdated, the record's source commit; migration `0036_module_index_summaries`, which drops the commits indexed before so they are indexed again). `GET /module-summaries` (the modules there; deleted ones still from their history), `GET /translations/status` and `GET /reports/translations` are filtered, ordered and paged on the index as the reader sees it, kept per commit and reading key (and, for summaries, the released commit and `filter[lang]`); summaries read only the page's files (`meta.text`, `attributes.variants`), the status none. So a warm list answers in milliseconds and a deep page costs what page 1 does. A commit not yet indexed (a save point, a merge) is carried over from the last index the server built or read: Git names the paths that differ (`ContentRepository.differingPaths`), unchanged files keep their rows, a translation is compared again when its source file or its record changed, and only those files are read (`updateCommitIndex`; a changed configuration builds from every file). `index-rebuild` builds each commit from the one before the same way.
- **Where used:** `meta.usedIn` on `GET /modules` and `GET /modules/:lang/:id` (`GetModuleUsageAction`): per module ID, in any language, the manuals including it (`direct: true` when an entry names it, `false` through module references) and the modules whose reference blocks name it (`{ kind, id, title, direct }`; manuals first, then modules, each by title; at most 50), and `meta.usage` (`manuals`, `modules`: all of them).

## Module history

Part of the `modules` component (#251; `GetModuleHistoryAction`, `CompareModuleVersionsAction`, `core/module-history.ts`). Derived from Git on every request, never stored: a version is a commit on the ref's first-parent line that changed the module's file (decided in #242), and revision numbers are counted, not kept.

- **`GET /modules/:lang/:id/history`** (`content.read`; `?ref=` as on every content route, default `main`; a maintenance line's branch works the same; an older commit or a draft branch needs `content.read.draft`, as reading it does): the commits among the newest 1,000 of the ref's first-parent line (`HISTORY_WINDOW`) that changed `modules/<lang>/<id>.adoc`, compared with their first parent (`GitRepo.pathHistory`, `content-repo`): a merge that brought a change of the file in is listed, a commit that changed only other files is not, and a save point made on a change set's branch is not listed on `main` by itself (its merge is). Newest first, paged. One `ModuleVersion` each (`id` `<lang>/<moduleId>/<commit>`):
  - `attributes`: `commit`, `committedAt` (the committer date), `message` (without the trailers; a merge's is the change set's title and a line naming it), `merge`, `actor` (`Merged-by`, else `Actor`: `{ kind, id }` or null) and `approvedBy` (the `Approved-by` trailers, in order), **as the commit claims them**: the server writes them on the commits it makes, but anyone who can push to the repository can write them too (see "Save points": nothing a commit says about itself counts for the workflow; here they are shown, for people);
  - `relationships`: `actor` (a `User` when the trailer names a user the directory knows, else empty), `changeSet` (`Merged-change-set`, else `Change-set`, when it is a change set's ID), `approvers` (the users among `approvedBy` the directory knows);
  - `meta`: `revision` (1 for the oldest, counting up; null when the line was cut at the window, `meta.truncated` on the document, while the file already existed before the oldest commit looked at, since the count would not start at the first; when the file began within the window it counts from there, so a file deleted and added again before the window starts its count anew, and a reader to whom the file before the window is hidden still gets null), `change` (`added`, `changed`, `removed`), `releases` (the release tags, of the 50 newest as for the status, whose commit holds exactly this file, by blob: `moduleRevision` as the reader sees it there with `content.read`) and `unreadable`.
- **As the reader sees it** (`ReadableContent.moduleHistory`): each version's file is read (by revision, parsed once) and judged by the ref's configuration and the reader's scope for the ref's permission, as reading the module there would. A revision they may not see is no file to them: a change from one hidden revision to another is no version, one that moves the module out of their scope is a removal, one that brings it back an addition, and their revision numbers count only what they see. A broken file is a version only for a reader of all of its language (`meta.unreadable`). A module whose file at the ref (for one deleted there: its last file) they may not see is **404**, exactly like a missing one; one that has no version they see is 404 too. A module deleted at the ref keeps its history (its newest version the removal), so it can be found and restored.
- **`GET /modules/:lang/:id/compare?base=<commit|tag>&head=<commit|tag>`** (`content.read`; both required, once each; no `?ref=`): each side is opened as reading it needs (`OpenSnapshotAction`): a released commit (`main`'s head, a tag) with `content.read`, any other, also an older commit of `main`, with `content.read.draft`, else 404 as for a ref that does not exist. A `ModuleComparison` (`id` `<lang>/<moduleId>/<base commit>..<head commit>`): `attributes.base` and `attributes.head`, the module on each side (`ModuleAttributes`, null where it is not there), and in `meta` the two commits and the diff in a `ChangeSetChange`'s shape (`change`: `added`, `changed`, `removed` or `unchanged`; `fields`; `blocks` by `diffBlocks`; `unreadable`), so the review screen's diff renders it unchanged. Each side shows the module only as the reader would see it on the other too (`seenOnBoth`, as a change set's diff); 404 when neither side has it.
- **Deleted modules and draft access** (#418, as #410 for deleted manuals): `GET /module-summaries?filter[status]=deleted` (or `any`) summarises a deleted module from the last versions of its files, which mostly sit in commits that are not released and which only `content.read.draft` reads (`readPermissionAt`). So for a reader without `content.read.draft`, and for one whose draft scopes would not show a file's last version alike (a narrower draft scope: `ReadableContent.asDrafts`, the same file by revision), a deleted file is listed only when released content (`openReleased`: `main`'s head, or one of the 50 newest release tags that are not withdrawn) holds exactly its last version (by blob, `ReadableContent.moduleWithFile`), and summarised from that file as they read it there (judged by that release's configuration and their scope): title, keywords, facets, variants and `meta.text` are what they could read anyway. A module none of whose deleted files is held is neither listed nor counted; one with some held lists only those languages. `meta.deleted` still names the deletion commit (commit metadata of the ref's line, as the history shows it). The route is `@BeyondTheCommit()`, so its ETag hashes the answer. A reader of drafts gets what #320 gives, unchanged. The editor's content browser disables "Show deleted" for a reader without draft access, with the reason.
- **Delivery API:** both routes are in it (`@DeliveryApi()`); a consumer holding `content.read` alone reads the history of released refs and compares released commits (two tags, a tag and `main`). The history names what the trailers of `main`'s commits name: the merger's and approvers' user IDs and the change set's ID (decided by #251's scope: who changed it, in which change set, approved by whom); a consumer reads no change set itself. Change sets reach `main` only as merge commits (a merge with approvals is never a fast-forward, and every merge has them), so no save point of a draft is listed on `main`.
- **Caching:** the history depends on the tags and the user directory, not only the commit read, so it is `@BeyondTheCommit()` (as the module routes); versions are kept per commit, reading key and module (`HISTORIES_KEPT`), path walks per commit and path in `content-repo`.
- **A renamed module** (#265, "Save points") has two histories: under its new ID from the rename on (the rename an `added` version), under its old ID up to it (its newest version the removal). Neither follows the other: Git keeps no rename, and a version's file is read by its path. So a version from before the rename is listed, and restored, only under the old ID: restoring it brings the old ID back as a module of its own (as any deleted module), never under the new ID; to take its text under the new ID, compare and copy it.
- **Restoring a version** needs no route of its own: read the old version (`GET /modules/:lang/:id?ref=<commit>` or the compare's `attributes.base`, which needs what reading that commit needs), then, in a change set (the open one, else `POST /change-sets`), lock the module (`POST /change-sets/:changeSetId/locks`), read its working copy (`GET …/modules/:lang/:moduleId/working-copy`, for `version` and `baseCommit`) and save the old content into it (`PUT …/working-copy`); a save point commits it. Discarding the working copy (`DELETE …/working-copy`) undoes it. A module deleted at the ref is restored the same way, as a subject with nothing committed (so only by an author who edits and reads all of its language; see "Locks").

## Manual history

Part of the `manuals` component (#321; `GetManualHistoryAction`, `CompareManualVersionsAction`), on the module history's pattern and code (`versionsSeen`, `HistoryLookups` in `modules/core/`): derived from Git on every request, never stored.

- **`GET /manuals/:id/history`** (`content.read`; `?ref=` as on every content route; an older commit or a draft branch needs `content.read.draft`; without it, only released versions, see "Draft access" below): the commits among the newest 1,000 of the ref's first-parent line that changed `manuals/<id>.yaml` (`Snapshot.manifestHistory`, `content-repo`), newest first, paged. One `ManualVersion` each (`id` `<manualId>/<commit>`), with exactly a `ModuleVersion`'s members (who, change set, approvers, `meta.revision`, `change`, `releases` by blob, `unreadable`).
- **As the reader sees it** (`ReadableContent.manualHistory`): each version's manifest is read by revision and judged by its audience, as reading the manual there would (`manual`); a broken one only for a reader of every audience. A version is the manifest **as the reader sees it** (naming only the modules they may see; a hash of that view, never the file's blob, also for the release badges), so a commit that changed only entries hidden from them is no version to them, and consecutive commits they see alike are one. A revision they may not see is no file to them (a removal, and an addition when it comes back); a manual whose last manifest they may not see is **404**, exactly like a missing one. A manual deleted at the ref keeps its history, its newest version the removal.
- **`GET /manuals/:id/compare?base=&head=`** (`content.read`; both required, once each): each side opened as reading it needs; a `ManualComparison` with `attributes.base` and `attributes.head` (`ManualAttributes`, null where it is not there) and in `meta` the two commits, `change` and `fields` (the manifest's members that differ, as in a change set's changes), so the review screen's manual diff draws it. Each side only as the reader would see it on the other too (`seenOnBoth`: the audience on both, and only the modules a manifest may name to them on both).
- **Deleted manuals** (`GET /manuals?filter[status]=deleted`, or `any` for both in one list ordered by ID): the manifests deleted on the ref's first-parent line among its newest 1,000 commits (`Snapshot.deletedManifests`, over `GitRepo.deletedFiles`) that are not there now, each from its last manifest, listed only when the reader may see that one (`ReadableContent.deletedManuals`, by its audience, naming only the modules they may see), with `meta.deleted` (its newest deletion). So a manual outside their scope is never listed or counted, and each one listed has a history.
- **Draft access** (#410): a version's manifest, and a deleted manual's last one, mostly sits in a commit that is not released, which only `content.read.draft` reads (`readPermissionAt`, as the compare's sides and the module history's). So for a reader without `content.read.draft`, and for one whose draft scopes would not show it alike (a narrower draft scope: `ReadableContent.asDrafts`, the same versions, or the same last manifest, read through it):
  - the history lists only the versions released content holds (`HistoryLookups.released`): `main`'s head, else a release tag (of the 50 newest, withdrawn ones aside, as for `releases`), holding exactly that manifest as they see it there; the commit read counts too when it is released (a withdrawn tag, or one older than those checked). A removal, a broken manifest (all alike, so never matched), and a version no released commit holds are no versions to them; the others keep their revision numbers (so numbers can skip); a manual with versions but none released is 404, as one they never saw. Every `ManualVersion` has `meta.readAt`, the commit to read it at (compare, `GET /manuals/:id?ref=`): its own commit for a reader of drafts, else that released commit, so nothing listed points them at a draft;
  - `filter[status]=deleted` lists a deleted manual only when released content (`main`'s head, a release tag) holds exactly its last manifest (by blob), and from that manifest as they read it there (`openReleased`, `ReadableContent.manualWithFile`: its audience judged by that release): what they could read anyway. The others are neither listed nor counted.
  - `GET /manuals` is `@BeyondTheCommit()` (an answer-hashed ETag, never `immutable`), as its deleted manuals depend on `main` and the releases. A listed history version still names its own commit and trailers, and keeps its revision number and `change` counted over every version (so numbers can skip): commit metadata of the ref's line, as the module history shows it to a consumer (#251); its content is what is withheld.
  - A reader of drafts gets what #321 gives, unchanged. The editor's builder disables "Show deleted" for a reader without draft access, with the reason; its history page lists what the API lists and says only released versions are shown.
  - The module history lists the commits of a released ref for a consumer, as decided in #251 (metadata only; reading a version needs what reading its commit needs).
- **Restoring a version** needs no route of its own, as for a module: read the old manifest (`GET /manuals/:id?ref=<commit>`), then, in a change set, lock the manual, read its working copy (`version`, `baseCommit`; for a deleted manual, 404, so the change set's `baseCommit`) and save the old manifest into it (`PUT …/manuals/:manualId/working-copy`): the save checks the restorer's scope as any edit (a manual whose committed manifest names modules they may not see is 403; a deleted one is a manual with nothing committed). Discarding the working copy (`DELETE …/working-copy`) undoes it.
- **Delivery API and caching:** both routes are in the delivery API; the history is `@BeyondTheCommit()` (releases, users), as a module's.

## Comments

The `comments` component (`src/comments/`, #24): reviewers discuss a change set on the exact block they mean; authors resolve the threads. Postgres state, never Git; every write goes to the audit log, in the same transaction.

- **The anchor** is a module in one language and a block ID: `subject` (`{ type: "module", lang, moduleId }`, as locks name it) and `blockId` (a block, list item or procedure step: every node `walk` yields). Never a position or text, so:
  - **edited** (same ID, other content): the comment stays on it;
  - **moved** (same ID elsewhere in the module, also into or out of a container): it stays on it;
  - **deleted** by a later save point (or the whole module removed): the comment stays, `meta.orphaned: true`. Derived on every read against the change set's head (the review head once submitted, else the branch's head; named in the list's `meta.head`), never stored, so a block that comes back with its ID un-orphans its comments. A module file at the head that cannot be read gives `false`.
  - Another **language** is another anchor: a translation's block shares the ID but not the comment.
  - Only committed content is anchored: a block that is only in a working copy (not yet in a save point) is `block-not-found`. A block the change set removed (at the base, not the head) may be commented on, and is orphaned from the start.
  - `commit` records the head the comment was written on.
- **Threads**: a comment without `relationships.thread` starts one (its `relationships.thread` names itself); a reply names the thread's first comment and gets its anchor (a reply to a reply is 422 `invalid-thread`). Only a thread is resolved and reopened; its replies go with it.
- **Table** `comments` (`repos/comments.tables.ts`, migration `0011_comments`): `id` (UUID version 7), `change_set_id`, `thread_id` (its own ID for a thread), `subject_type`, `subject_id` (`<lang>/<moduleId>`), `block_id`, `commit`, `author_kind`/`author_id`, `body` (plain text, trimmed, 1 to 10,000 characters; null exactly when deleted), `created_at`, `edited_at`, `deleted_at` (only on a thread's first comment; migration `0027_comment_edits`), `resolved_at`/`resolved_by_kind`/`resolved_by_id` (all set or all null, and only on a thread). A composite foreign key holds a reply to its thread's change set and anchor; that it names a thread's first comment, not a reply, is held by the action only. A change set holds at most 10,000 comments (409 `too-many-comments`, `meta.max`).
- **Who sees them** follows the change set's own visibility: the change set is found as `GET /change-sets/:changeSetId` finds it (another author's is 404 for an actor without `content.read.draft`, exactly like a missing one), and a comment is there for a reader who sees its module (`CommentAnchors`, `core/comment-anchors.ts`): at the head where the module is there (with the head's read permission), else at the base; a module on neither side any more only for a reader of all of its language on both. Any other is not there, exactly like a missing one: not listed nor counted (filtered before paging), 404 by ID, 422 `invalid-thread` as a reply's thread, 422 `block-not-found` as a new thread's module. Every route needs `content.read` (they read content).
- **Who writes them** (a thread, a reply, resolving, reopening): the change set's author, or a holder of `content.review` in a role covering the module (`moduleTarget` of the module where it is found; on neither side, a role covering all of its language); 403 `forbidden` otherwise, one answer whatever the reason. Only on an open change set (`draft`, `in-review`, `approved`): else 409 `invalid-state` with `meta.state`; the comments of a merged or abandoned one stay readable while its sides open (its branch is there; for an author without `content.read.draft`, while the branch has not moved past the review head, as for its changes). Each write holds the change set's row (`FOR UPDATE`, bounded: 503 `database-timeout`) from the state check to the end.

| Route                                                        | What                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| ------------------------------------------------------------ | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `GET /change-sets/:changeSetId/comments`                     | paged, in thread order (threads by when started, each followed by its replies, oldest first); `filter[module]` (`<lang>/<moduleId>`), `filter[block]`, `filter[resolved]` (`false` by default: resolved threads hidden; `true`; `any`)        |
| `GET /change-sets/:changeSetId/comments/:commentId`          | one                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |
| `POST /change-sets/:changeSetId/comments`                    | `{ data: { type: "Comment", attributes: { body, subject, blockId } } }` for a thread, or `{ data: { type: "Comment", attributes: { body }, relationships: { thread: { data: { type: "Comment", id } } } } }` for a reply; 201 with `Location` |
| `POST /change-sets/:changeSetId/comments/:commentId/resolve` | resolves a thread (422 `not-a-thread` for a reply, 409 `already-resolved`); 200 with the comment                                                                                                                                              |
| `POST /change-sets/:changeSetId/comments/:commentId/reopen`  | reopens it (409 `not-resolved`); 200 with the comment                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| `PATCH /change-sets/:changeSetId/comments/:commentId`        | `{ data: { type: "Comment", id, attributes: { body } } }`: one's own comment's new text (409 `comment-deleted`, `id-mismatch`); 200 with the comment                                                                                          |
| `DELETE /change-sets/:changeSetId/comments/:commentId`       | deletes one's own comment (409 `comment-deleted`); 204                                                                                                                                                                                        |

- A `Comment` has `attributes` `subject`, `blockId`, `body` (plain text; null once deleted), `author`, `commit`, `createdAt`, `resolvedAt` and `resolvedBy` (null while open and always on a reply), `relationships.changeSet` and `relationships.thread`, and `meta.orphaned`, `meta.deleted` and `meta.editedAt` (null while never edited).
- **Editing and deleting** (#268; `EditCommentAction`, `DeleteCommentAction`): only the comment's **author**, while they may still write comments on its module (above): 403 `forbidden` for anyone else who sees it, 404 exactly like a missing one for anyone who does not. Nobody edits or deletes another person's comment, an admin included: there is no moderation. Only on an open change set (409 `invalid-state`), without a time limit; a deleted comment is 409 `comment-deleted`. Each holds the change set's row and the comment's, as the other writes do.
  - **An edit** replaces the text and sets `edited_at`; the comment keeps its ID, anchor, thread and resolution. **No history of the text:** the old text is not kept by the API (the audit log never holds a comment's text); the `comment.edit` event says that, when and by whom. The same text again changes nothing and is not audited.
  - **A delete** removes the text from the table and the API (the database's own dead rows, write-ahead log and backups keep it until vacuum and their retention end). A thread's first comment **with replies** stays in its place, `meta.deleted: true`, `body` null (the editor shows "Comment deleted"), so the replies stay readable; its author and time stay, and it may still be resolved, reopened and replied to. It still counts towards the 10,000 comments of a change set. A **reply**, or a thread **without replies**, is removed (404 from then on; not listed). The last reply of a deleted thread takes the thread's first comment with it, so nothing is left of a thread nobody can read. A reply racing the removal of its thread is 422 `invalid-thread`.
  - **Notifications** (#252, #319) never carried a comment or its text, and comments write no outbox rows, so an edit or a delete has nothing to take back there; the same holds for webhooks.
- **Audit:** `comment.create` (`data: { changeSetId, module, blockId, commit, threadId? }`), `comment.resolve`, `comment.reopen` and `comment.edit` (`data: { changeSetId }`), `comment.delete` (`data: { changeSetId, threadId?, kept, threadRemoved? }`: `kept` when it stays as deleted, `threadRemoved` when the last reply of a deleted thread took it along), each with the comment as subject (`filter[subject]=comment/<id>`). The text is never in the audit log (it may hold personal data): the table holds only the current text, and none once deleted.

## Outbox

The `outbox` component (`src/outbox/`): the events other components write in the transaction of the change they report (`outbox`, `change-sets/repos/outbox.tables.ts`), read in order by named consumers, each with its cursor in `outbox_consumers`: `notifications` and `webhooks` (`OutboxFeed`, see "Notifications"), and the change feed (`ChangeFeedSource`, see "Change feed"), whose row holds only its latch, as its readers keep their own positions.

- **Order:** `(tx_id, id)`, only below the settled horizon (`pg_snapshot_xmin(pg_current_snapshot())`), so a cursor never steps over a row committed late. This holds only for transaction IDs of this database server.
- **Blocked after a restore** (#619): a database restored onto another Postgres server (a new stack from a backup, a move to another host) starts a new transaction count there, so the restored rows and cursors lie past every new transaction, and a new event would sort before events a consumer has passed: it would be skipped, silently. So before each read, a consumer whose cursor, or any outbox row, lies at or past `pg_snapshot_xmax(pg_current_snapshot())` (no transaction of this server has such an ID) is latched `blocked` (`outbox_consumers.blocked`, migration `0053_outbox_restore`) and reads nothing: no notification, no webhook delivery, and `GET /changes` answers 503 `outbox-blocked`. Each process logs it once at error level (`NotificationsConsumer`, `WebhooksConsumer`, `ChangeFeed`), and Admin, System shows it in its Background work card (`GET /outbox/status`, `admin.roles`: the blocked consumers' names) with a link to the user guide's page on it (`guide/self-hosting/after-a-restore`, the same recipe). The latch holds, once set, even after this server's count has caught up (the restored positions still mean nothing here), until the recipe below clears it. The audit log's sink has the same latch (`audit_sink.blocked`, "Audit log", "After a restore"); after a restore both recipes are run.
- **After a restore** onto another database server: run this once, as the database owner, best right after `pg_restore` and before the first start, else with the API stopped (`docker compose stop api worker`), as a script (`psql -f` or on stdin: `VACUUM` cannot run inside the transaction a single `psql -c` makes). It needs nothing from you and does no harm to notifications and webhooks on a database that was not restored (every position is put on the same count), but every change-feed reader then resyncs (below). For a backup from before `0053_outbox_restore`, start once first so the migrations run, then stop the API and the worker and run it. Every row takes transaction 1, in ID order (so the rows a consumer had not handled come in ID order, not their old transaction order); each cursor stands before the first row it had not handled and never past its old place, so the rows recorded since the restore (higher IDs: the identity sequence is restored with the rows) are delivered too, and at worst a few are read again, which every consumer takes once while its record lasts (a notification is unique per row and recipient, a webhook delivery per subscriber and idempotency key; one pruned meanwhile is made again, and a receiver dedupes it by its `Idempotency-Key`):

  ```sql
  -- outbox: after a restore
  BEGIN;
  UPDATE outbox_consumers SET last_tx_id = '1',
    last_outbox_id = LEAST((SELECT min(o.id) - 1 FROM outbox o
                            WHERE (o.tx_id, o.id) > (outbox_consumers.last_tx_id, outbox_consumers.last_outbox_id)),
                           outbox_consumers.last_outbox_id)
    WHERE last_tx_id <> '0';
  UPDATE outbox SET tx_id = '1';
  UPDATE outbox_consumers SET blocked = false;
  COMMIT;
  VACUUM outbox;
  ```

  A restore drill (a second stack from a backup, `deploy/README.md`, "Restore drill") skips the recipe, so it stays held (usually: only when the restored positions lie past the drill server's count, as on a fresh Postgres), and turns its webhook subscribers off (`UPDATE webhooks SET active = false`) so it never sends to the receivers of the stack it copies.

  The change feed's readers held cursors from before the restore: those now answer 410 `cursor-gone` (the row a cursor stands on no longer has the position it names), and they resync. `outbox-restore.spec.ts` runs this very block, read from this file.

## Notifications

The `notifications` component (`src/notifications/`, #252), over the `outbox` component (`src/outbox/`): each person has an inbox of what concerns them, filled from the outbox without anyone polling the screens.

- **The outbox reader** (`OutboxFeed`, `src/outbox/`; shared with webhooks, #42, as a second consumer): each consumer has a cursor in `outbox_consumers` (`name`, `last_tx_id`, `last_outbox_id`, `updated_at`; migration `0014_notifications`), made at the start of the outbox on its first read. Rows are read in `(tx_id, id)` order, and only those whose transaction is older than every one still running (`pg_snapshot_xmin(pg_current_snapshot())`): an `id` is taken when a row is inserted, not when its transaction commits, so a cursor on `id` alone could step over a row committed late, for good. `outbox.tx_id` (`pg_current_xact_id()`, the top-level transaction, 64 bits, never wrapping) is new in the same migration. A transaction left open (anywhere in the cluster) delays the rows after it; it never makes them skipped. A consumer moves its cursor in the transaction that writes what it made of a batch, by compare-and-swap on where it read from, so a batch is recorded once whoever reads it; what it writes must be idempotent per row. `outbox.delivered_at` stays unused.
- **The consumer** (`NotificationsConsumer`, `api/`) runs `DeliverNotificationsAction` at start-up and every `CCMS_NOTIFICATIONS_POLL_MS` (default 3 s; 0: not on a timer, as in tests, which call `runOnce`), one pass at a time, at most 10 batches of 100 rows and 20 s per pass. The fan-out of a batch reads Git and the user directory outside any transaction; then one bounded transaction moves the cursor and stores the notifications. A restart mid-batch writes nothing of it, the next run reads it again, and a notification is unique per outbox row and recipient (`notifications_outbox_recipient`), so nobody hears twice; servers side by side may each run it (the loser of the cursor's compare-and-swap drops its batch). A row whose fan-out fails stops its batch there, after recording the rows before it: an outage (`UnavailableError`) is tried again however long it lasts, a domain error (a commit or change set not there, too many changes) passes the row over at once, anything else after 5 tries in the process; a payload that is not what its topic writes notifies nobody. All of it is logged.
- **Who hears of what** (`FanOut`, `core/fan-out.ts`; defaults from #242): `change-set.submit`: the holders of `content.review` whose scope covers everything the change set changes at the submitted head (`coversChanges`, as approving needs) and who read all of it as a draft (`coversChanges` with `content.read.draft`), so nobody hears of what they may not read; `change-set.return`, `approve`, `sign-off`, `merge` and `lock.break`: the change set's author (a lock's holder); `translation.imported` and `translation.import-failed` (#51): the importer named in the payload; `release.output-failed` (#249) and `release.output-recovered` (#551): holders of `release.publish` over the manual's audience at the release's commit; `release.published` (#138), a `major` one only: every reader (`content.read`) of its manuals' audiences, each told only of the manuals they read. **Never the actor who caused it**, only people (users), never a service. People are the user directory's users with a role (at most 10,000), read once per pass. The content is read as `service:notifications` (reads every ref; never answers a request). `release.published` is written since #138 (see "Releases"; its `manualIds` may be empty). `translation.imported` is written since #51 (see "Translation imports"); `translation.import-failed` is not written yet; `release.output-failed` is written since #249 (with its `commit` since #551) and `release.output-recovered` since #551 (see "Releases", "Outputs"). Their payloads, as the fan-out reads them: `{ importId, importer: { kind, id }, actor, changeSetId? }` (a background import names its job as `actor`, so the importer hears of it; a translation import adds `jobId`, `languages`, `modules` (up to 1,000 `{ lang, moduleId }`) and `moduleCount`, and since #656 `designs` (up to 1,000 `{ lang, designId }`), `designCount`, `manuals` (up to 1,000 `{ lang, manualId }`) and `manualCount`), `{ releaseId, manualIds, commit, level: "major" | "minor" | "patch", actor }`, `{ releaseId, manualId, commit, actor? }`.
- **What a notification says** (table `notifications`, `repos/notifications.tables.ts`): `recipient_id` (a user's ID), `kind` (the topic), `subject_type` and `subject_id` (`change-set`, `release` or `import`: what it opens), `payload`, `created_at` (when the event happened: its outbox row's time), `read_at`, `outbox_id`. The payload holds only what a client builds its sentence from and the recipient may read: IDs, a change set's `title`, `by` (who did it), `state` (where it went), `reason` (`head-moved`), a lock break's `count` and up to 20 `subjects`, a release's `level` and `manualIds`. Never a comment's text, never server-side English (the editor's texts are its own translation keys, #90).
- **Routes** (any actor; nobody reads or marks another person's notifications, and a service, the API token, has none): `GET /me/notifications` (paged, newest first, `filter[unread]=true|false`, `meta.unreadCount` whatever the filter; `Cache-Control: private, no-store`); `GET /me/notifications/unread-count` (`{ meta: { unreadCount, latest } }`, for the bell: one indexed query, an `ETag` from the recipient, the count and the newest notification's ID, so a poll with `If-None-Match` is a 304 while nothing changed); `GET /me/notifications/:notificationId` (another person's, a malformed ID or none: 404 alike); `POST /me/notifications/read` with `{ meta: { ids } }` (1 to 200) or `{ meta: { all: true } }`: an ID that is not the caller's is 404 and nothing is marked; one read already keeps its time; answers `{ meta: { marked, unreadCount } }`.
- **Retention:** notifications read more than `CCMS_NOTIFICATIONS_KEEP_DAYS` ago (default 90; by when they were read, so one read today is kept that long however old its event) are deleted by the consumer at start-up and hourly, 1,000 at a time and at most 20,000 per run; unread ones are kept.
- Out of scope: e-mail and push, settings per person or kind (with #222's preferences), the editor's sheet (#90).

## Secrets

The `secrets` component (`src/secrets/`, #470): one **instance secret key** seals every secret the server stores and must use itself (webhook signing secrets and webhook header secrets, #457, a queued email's parameters, #609, now; client secrets later), so no admin has to make a key or edit a settings file for webhooks to work.

- **The port** `SecretSealer` (`base/core/secret-sealer.ts`, global, so any component injects it): `seal(secret, context)` and `open(sealed, context)`, `available` false only when there is no key. The context names what the secret is and whose (`webhook-secret:v1:<ID>`): a value opens only for the context it was sealed for. The adapter (`base/repos/aes-secret-sealer.ts`): AES-256-GCM, `v1.<iv>.<ciphertext>.<tag>` (base64url), a fresh 96-bit IV per seal, `ccms:<context>` as the additional authenticated data; the format webhook secrets had before, so they still open. **A component that stores sealed values** also implements `SealedSecretSource` (`sample` a few, `reseal` all in a transaction) and lists it in `src/secrets/sealed-secret-sources.ts`, so the start-up check and the rotation cover them.
- **Where the key comes from** (`OpenSecretKeyAction`, run before any request or job by `SecretKeyGate`): `CCMS_SECRET_KEY` when set (or its old name `CCMS_WEBHOOKS_SECRET_KEY`, deprecated, with a warning; both set to different keys is an invalid setting); else the key file (`CCMS_SECRET_KEY_FILE`); else, unless `CCMS_SECRET_KEY_GENERATE=false`, a new key of 32 bytes from the CSPRNG, written as the key file. Else none: webhooks are off.
- **The key file** (`repos/fs-secret-key-file.ts`): by default `ccms-secret.key` in the folder that holds the content repository (`CCMS_CONTENT_DIR`; locally `.local/ccms-secret.key`). That folder is the one local, writable place every installation has, whichever blob store it uses (`CCMS_BLOB_FS_ROOT` exists only with the `fs` driver, and a blob folder may move to S3), and it is backed up with the content already; beside the repository, not in it, so Git never carries the key with a clone or a push. The container image keeps the content repository and the key file on one data volume (`/data`, `deploy/README.md`). The file holds the key as base64 and a newline (an operator can copy it into `CCMS_SECRET_KEY`), mode 0600, its folder made with 0700 when missing. **Made once:** written whole and synced under a temporary name, then hard-linked to its name, which fails when the name exists: two servers starting at once never make two keys (the second takes the first's), and none reads half a file. A file others may read is used, with a warning.
- **Never in the database:** a dump of the database alone (a backup, a replica, a leak) opens none of the secrets. Nor is it logged, answered by the API (`GET /secret-key` says only where it is) or written to the audit log; `SecretKey` prints as `[secret key]`.
- **The key check** (table `secret_key_check`, migration `0034_secret_key_check`; `repos/drizzle-secret-key-check.ts`): one row, a fixed text sealed under the key (never the key, nor anything that gives it away), written by the first start that has a key and re-sealed by a rotation. **Refusing to start** (a clear message, never a new key): when the key does not open the check (another key set, the wrong file mounted), or there is a check (or sealed secrets) but no key: the key file lost (no new key is made then, so restoring the backup is enough), or a second server with a key file of its own that would make another key. A database from before the check is judged by its sealed values (5 of each kind are tried, one must open), then gets its check. One damaged value does not stop the server (its delivery fails as `secret-unreadable`).
- **One at a time:** a start opens the key, and a rotation runs, in a transaction holding the key's advisory lock (`pg_advisory_xact_lock`), so a server starting during a rotation waits for it, and two rotations never interleave.
- **Several servers, hosting:** set `CCMS_SECRET_KEY` to the same key on every server (or share one key file), and `CCMS_SECRET_KEY_GENERATE=false` where a missing key must never be made up. The default key file is for one server, or several on one volume.
- **Rotation:** stop every server, workers included (`CCMS_ROLE=worker`: they deliver the webhooks; in compose `docker compose stop api worker`), then `pnpm nx run api:rotate-secret-key` (`node backend/api/dist/secrets/rotate-secret-key.main.js`; `RotateSecretKeyAction`): under the lock, checks the current key as a start does, then re-seals every stored secret and the check under a new key in one transaction. A value the current key does not open was unreadable already: it is left as it is and counted (give that subscriber a new secret); a webhook's previous secret past its end is cleared instead. With the key file the new key is made, written beside it as `<file>.next`, and renamed over the file after the commit (only when `.next` still holds this rotation's key). A rotation cut off before the commit leaves the old key working: the next start sets `.next` aside as `<file>.next.discarded.<time>.<id>` (a name of its own each time, never deleted: remove them once a backup is no longer needed); one cut off after it is finished by the next start (`.next` opens the check). With `CCMS_SECRET_KEY`, set `CCMS_SECRET_KEY_NEW` to the new key for the command, then `CCMS_SECRET_KEY` to it. The secrets themselves do not change: receivers notice nothing. Never prints a key. A key file that is a symbolic link (a mounted secret) is replaced by a plain file: rotate such a key with `CCMS_SECRET_KEY_NEW` and update the mount instead.
- **What to back up:** the key file (or wherever `CCMS_SECRET_KEY` comes from) together with the database: one without the other loses every stored secret. After a rotation, back the key file up again.
- **Admin, System** (`GET /secret-key`, `admin.roles`): `origin` (`file`, `environment` or `none`), the key file's path for `file`, the variable for `environment`, and whether generation is on. Never the key.

## Mail

The `mail` component (`src/mail/`, #609): branded email through the installation's own SMTP server, for sign-in links and other messages (#610 is the first user). Off without a mail server; nothing else depends on it.

- **Settings** (`src/config/mail-env.ts`, `.env.example`, `deploy/README.md` "Email"): `CCMS_MAIL_URL` (`smtp://host[:port]`, 587 with STARTTLS, or `smtps://host[:port]`, 465 with TLS from the start; never credentials, a path or a query in it), `CCMS_MAIL_FROM` (a bare address, required with the URL), `CCMS_MAIL_USER` with `CCMS_MAIL_PASSWORD` or `CCMS_MAIL_PASSWORD_FILE` (a mounted secret, its last newline dropped; the file wins), `CCMS_MAIL_REQUIRE_TLS` (default true: STARTTLS or nothing is sent; false only for a relay on a trusted network), `CCMS_MAIL_CA_FILE` (a private CA's certificates, trusted in place of the system's), `CCMS_MAIL_LANGUAGE` (`en` or `de`, default `en`: the language of mail to someone without one).
- **States** (`MailSettings.state`): `off` (no URL), `no-secret-key` (a URL, but no instance secret key: a queued email's parameters are sealed, so mail stays off; logged once at start-up and shown in Admin › System) and `on`.
- **Sending** (`MailOutbox.queue(tx, { to, template, params, language })`, exported by `MailModule`): in the caller's transaction, checks the address and the template's parameters, stores the email in `mail_messages` (migration `0051_mail`) with its parameters sealed (`mail-params:v1:<id>`; `MailSealedSecretSource` covers them in the key check and the rotation) and enqueues its job (`mail.send`, keyed by the email's ID). Off, it answers null and stores nothing. The job renders the template with the branding at send time and sends; the row is removed once sent or given up, and each email sent clears rows older than two days (one whose job was lost). Retries: 6, every 30 s growing; a 5xx reply is final at once. Giving up writes `mail.failed` (by `service:mail`: the template, the reason `rejected`, `unavailable`, `tls`, `auth`, `off` or `unreadable`, the attempts; never the address, the parameters or the server's reply).
- **The email** (`core/templates/`): a template is a Zod schema for its parameters and its texts per language; the layout writes an HTML part (tables and inline styles, the branding's light logo embedded as a PNG `cid:logo`, never a remote image; the primary colour on the button only when it has 4.5:1 contrast with its text) and a plain-text part. Every value is HTML-escaped; links are only http(s) and only under `CCMS_PUBLIC_URL`. The sender's name is the branding's product name (or the default one). Header injection: names and the subject go through `headerText` (control characters and line breaks become spaces, cut to a length), addresses through `isMailAddress` (no whitespace, quotes, angle brackets, commas or semicolons). A fixed, translated footer says who sent it and why the reader gets it. Headers `Auto-Submitted: auto-generated` and `Content-Language`.
- **The SMTP adapter** (`repos/smtp-mailer.ts`, nodemailer): TLS 1.2 at least, certificates checked against the host name, `requireTLS` unless turned off (so the password is only ever sent over TLS; with it off, a server without STARTTLS gets it in plain text); no file or URL access for attachments; time limits on connecting, greeting and the socket. An error is classified by its code and the reply's status, never the reply's text.
- **Admin › System** (`admin.roles`): `GET /mail` (the state, host, port, TLS mode, whether it signs in, the sender and the default language; never the user or the password) and `POST /mail/test` (202: queues the test email to the signed-in user, in the language asked for or the default; 404 `not-configured` with `meta.reason` `off` or `no-secret-key`; 422 `no-email-address` for someone without one; audited as `mail.test`).
- **Tests**: unit tests against `tests/fakes/fake-mail.ts`; the e2e tests send through an SMTP server in the test process (`tests/mail-server.ts`, `smtp-server` with a certificate made for the run): STARTTLS with sign-in, a server without STARTTLS (nothing sent, nothing said in plain text) and mail off.

## Webhooks

The `webhooks` component (`src/webhooks/`, #42): systems that want to hear of releases, deprecations and imports register a URL and are sent small, signed events, retried until they arrive, without polling. The editor's screen is Admin, Webhooks (#254).

- **Subscribers** (`webhook.manage`, a global permission; every change audited): `GET /webhooks` (paged, oldest first; each with `meta.lastDelivery`, its newest delivery's event, status, attempts, status code, error and time, or null), `GET /webhooks/:webhookId`, `POST /webhooks` (`url`, `events`, `description`, `maxAudience`, `headers`), `PATCH /webhooks/:webhookId` (`url`, `events`, `description`, `maxAudience`, `headers`; `active: false` pauses, `active: true` resumes, also one disabled for failing), `DELETE /webhooks/:webhookId` (with its log; waiting deliveries are not sent), `POST /webhooks/:webhookId/secret` (rotate; only the owner or a manager covering any owner of any subscriber, whatever its events, else 403 `forbidden`: the secret signs what the owner's receiver trusts), `GET /webhooks/:webhookId/deliveries` (the delivery log, paged, newest first, `filter[status]`, `filter[event]`; a delivery's `payload` was filtered by the scope of the owner it was made for (recorded on the delivery), so it is shown only to that owner or to a reader whose `content.read` restricts nothing (for `translation.imported` also `content.read.draft` and `translation.manage`), and is null for any other manager, who still sees its state, attempts and errors). A subscriber itself (URL, events, owner, state, log) is seen only by its owner or by a manager whose scope covers any owner of one with its events (`coversAnyOwner`: `content.read` restricting nothing; for `translation.imported` also `content.read.draft` and `translation.manage`); to anyone else it does not exist (404, left out of the list). Its events and its audience limit are changed by someone else only when they cover the old and the new events (else 403 `forbidden`), so nobody reshapes them to see more. At most 100 subscribers (409 `too-many-webhooks`). Audit events: `webhook.create` (host, events, the audience limit and the custom headers' names), `webhook.update` (the fields changed, the host when the URL changed, the custom headers changed by name; the new owner and the deliveries skipped when it moved), `webhook.delete`, `webhook.rotate-secret`, `webhook.test` (the delivery), `webhook.retry` (the delivery and its event), and `webhook.disable` by `service:webhooks`; never a secret, never the URL's path or query (it may hold a receiver's token).
- **On by default:** the secrets are sealed under the instance secret key (see "Secrets"), which the server makes on its first start. Only without one (`CCMS_SECRET_KEY_GENERATE=false` and none set) are the routes that make a secret 404 `not-configured` (the editor says webhooks are off) and nothing is delivered (the outbox cursor stays, so nothing is lost meanwhile).
