- **Custom headers** (`headers`, #457): a subscriber can send headers of its own with every delivery, for a receiver that authenticates requests its own way (`Authorization: Bearer …`, `X-Api-Key`): `{ name, value, secret }` on `POST` and `PATCH` (all of them: they replace those stored; left out of a `PATCH`, they stay). A **secret** one's value is stored sealed (`SecretSealer`, bound to `webhook-header:v1:<the subscriber's ID>:<the name in lower case>`, so it opens neither on another row nor as another header) and is **write-only**: every answer shows it as `{ name, secret: true, value: null }` (no part of it, not even the last characters), and no log, audit event or delivery record holds it; the editor shows "Set" with Replace and Remove. Given again without a `value`, a secret header keeps its stored value; but **a new URL needs every secret value again** (or the header removed; 422 `header-value-required`): a secret is never sent to a receiver it was not given for. Changing someone else's headers needs what rotating their secret does (the owner, or a manager whose scope restricts nothing; 403 `forbidden`); handing them back unchanged is no change. Checked in `core/webhook-headers.ts`: names RFC 9110 tokens of at most 100 characters, compared without case, none twice; values visible ASCII with spaces or tabs inside (never CR, LF, NUL or another control character, so no header can be smuggled in; leading and trailing white space dropped); at most 20, names and values together at most 8,192 characters (`GET /webhooks` gives these three limits in `meta.headerLimits`, so a client checks a form by the same numbers, #560). **Refused names** (what the server sets, or what would break the request's framing, its connection or the signature): `Host`, `Content-Length`, `Content-Type`, `Content-Encoding`, `Transfer-Encoding`, `Connection`, `Keep-Alive`, `TE`, `Trailer`, `Upgrade`, `Expect`, `Cookie`, `User-Agent`, `Idempotency-Key`, `Proxy-*` and `X-Ccms-*` (the signature among them). Every problem at once: 422, one error each with its own `code` (`header-name-invalid`, `header-name-refused`, `header-name-duplicate`, `header-value-invalid`, `header-value-required`, `too-many-headers`, `headers-too-large`) and `source.pointer`. Sent on every attempt, retries and the test event too, as they are when it is made, before the server's own (which no custom one can override); the signature stays over the body alone. A secret one that cannot be opened (the key changed) fails the attempt as `secret-unreadable` and sends nothing. A delivery records the **names** of the headers its last attempt sent (`sentHeaders`), never a value. Audit: `webhook.create` names them (`{ name, secret }`), `webhook.update` says what changed by name (`headers: { added, removed, changed, secretChanged }`), never a value. Rotating the instance key re-seals them too (`WebhookHeaderSealedSecretSource`, "webhook header secrets").
- **The secret** (`whsec_` and 64 hex digits, 256 bits from the CSPRNG) is answered **once**, in `meta.secret` of `POST /webhooks` and of a rotation (`Cache-Control: no-store`). It is stored sealed (`SecretSealer` under the instance secret key, see "Secrets": AES-256-GCM, a fresh IV each time, bound to `webhook-secret:v1:<the subscriber's ID>` so a sealed value copied onto another row does not open; `repos/sealed-webhook-secrets.ts`), since signing needs the secret itself; no read, list, log or audit event holds it. **Rotation:** the secret before keeps signing beside the new one for a day (`meta.previousSecretUntil`): the receiver switches when it likes. Rotating the instance key re-seals the stored secrets and changes none of them (receivers notice nothing).
- **Who hears of what:** a subscriber is owned by whoever made it or last pointed it at another URL (so nobody points another's subscriber, and what they may read, at a receiver of their own). Pointing someone else's subscriber elsewhere needs a scope covering any owner of one with its old and new events, else 403 `forbidden`: a narrower manager would silently narrow what it receives. Changing only its events, its description or its state keeps the owner, so a wider manager cannot widen what goes to a receiver someone else chose. When the owner changes, the deliveries still pending, made for the old owner, are skipped (`skipped`), and an attempt never sends a delivery made for another owner than the current one. The secret stays (the old owner's receiver knows it), so a new owner rotates it. Lookups go over DNS (c-ares, cancelled with the attempt), not the system resolver: `/etc/hosts` is not read. A subscriber is sent only events about content they may read, judged when each event is made, as they read it now (an owner deactivated, or no longer holding `webhook.manage`: nothing, and what was made for them and still waits is skipped). Events of the outbox (`OutboxFeed`, consumer `webhooks`, beside the notifications' cursor), read by `WebhooksConsumer` every `CCMS_WEBHOOKS_POLL_MS` (`FanOutWebhooksAction`; at most 10 batches of 50 rows and 20 s per run, the budget checked per row; a row whose reading is refused for one owner, such as content they cannot read, skips only that subscriber), only those written after the subscriber was made:
  - `manual.released`: one per manual a release changed as the owner sees it (`ReleaseChangeReader`, exactly what `GET /releases/{id}/changes` answers them: a manual outside their scope is not there), with its languages and their `change`; past `too-many-changes`, every manual they read, `change: null`, `complete: false`;
  - `module.released`: one per module and language a release changed (each module a changed manual names), with `change` and `contentHash` (the file's revision at the release; null when removed), at most 1,000 per release;
  - `release.withdrawn`: to an owner who reads released content anywhere (every reader may know a release), with the reason;
  - `module.deprecated` (written on merge, #266: `{ moduleId, lang?, commit, reason?, replacedBy? }`): when the owner may read the module there; `replacedBy` only when they may read the replacement there too, in some language (else null), so no subscriber learns of a module outside their scope (#43);
  - `manual.deprecated` (written on merge, #266, sent since #558: `{ manualId, commit, reason?, replacedBy? }`): when the owner may read the manual there (its audience, judged as notifications judge it; a manifest that cannot be read counts as the most restricted level); `replacedBy` only when they may read the replacement manual there too (else null); keyed over the manual and the commit;
  - `translation.imported` (written by a translation import's save point, #51: `{ importId, changeSetId }` of its payload): to an owner holding `translation.manage` and `content.read.draft` (an import lands in a change set, a draft).
    Drafts, reviews, the workflow and locks never fire one. A row whose payload is not what its topic writes makes nothing (logged); an outage is tried again.
- **Audience limit** (`maxAudience`, optional, an audience level's ID from the configuration, #254): narrows what the owner's roles let through to that level and the less restricted ones, for `manual.released`, `module.released`, `module.deprecated` and `manual.deprecated` (judged with the configuration at the event's commit); a level the configuration no longer holds lets nothing through. Each delivery records the limit it was built under: when the limit changes, the deliveries still pending are skipped, an attempt never sends one built under another limit, and one is not retried by hand. `release.withdrawn` and `translation.imported` name no content and are not limited.
- **A test event** (`POST /webhooks/:webhookId/test`, #254): sends a signed `ping` at once (`{ "type": "ping", "idempotencyKey", "occurredAt", "data": { "webhookId" } }`) and answers its delivery (`succeeded` or `failed`, the status code, the error); it is in the log as `ping`. It goes through the same sender as every delivery (the address checked after resolving the host, no redirect, the timeout, the signature), also to a paused or disabled subscriber (to try a receiver before resuming it), once: never retried, and never counted toward disabling it, though a failure starts the receiver's wait like any. While the subscriber has an attempt in flight, waits after a failure, or its owner has as many in flight as allowed, it is 429 `rate-limited` with `Retry-After`, and nothing is sent or recorded. Its body is shown as any delivery's (to the owner, or a manager covering any owner's).
- **Retry by hand** (`POST /webhooks/:webhookId/deliveries/:deliveryId/retry`, #254): a `failed` delivery back to `pending` with a new job and its attempts, under every rule a delivery follows; only of a subscriber still active, still subscribed to its event and owned by whom it was made for, else 409 `not-retryable` with `meta.reason` (`status`, `test-event`, `inactive`, `owner-changed`, `audience-changed`, `not-subscribed`). Its attempts add up; the two-hour put-off limit counts from when it was made, so an old delivery retried spends its attempts when the receiver is busy rather than waiting.
- **A delivery** (`webhook_deliveries`, unique per subscriber and idempotency key) is made with its job (`webhook.deliver`) in one transaction, before the cursor moves: an outbox batch read twice (a restart, two servers) makes it once. The body, stored and sent the same on every attempt: `{ "type", "idempotencyKey", "occurredAt", "data" }`, IDs and what changed, never content. Headers: `Content-Type: application/json`, `User-Agent: openfolia-webhooks/1`, `X-Ccms-Delivery` (the delivery's ID), `X-Ccms-Event`, `Idempotency-Key` and `X-Ccms-Signature`, after the subscriber's custom headers (above). **Idempotency keys** are SHA-256 hex: `manual.released` over the release and the manual, `module.released` over the release, the module, its language and its content hash, the others over what they name, so the same event always has the same key.
- **Retries:** a 2xx answer succeeds, and the delivery is never sent again. Anything else (a 3xx too: no redirect is followed; a timeout; a broken connection) is recorded and retried by the job: 9 attempts, 30 s apart at first, doubled each time with jitter (about two hours in all), then `failed`. A refused address or URL fails at once. After 5 deliveries in a row failed for good at the receiver (webhooks off, or a secret that cannot be opened, are ours and not counted) the subscriber is disabled (`active: false`, `meta.disabledReason: "failing"`, audited); `PATCH` `active: true` turns it on again. A paused or disabled subscriber's waiting deliveries are `skipped`. **No receiver or manager holds every sender:** one attempt at a time per subscriber, at most 2 at a time across one owner's subscribers (all servers, judged by the database's clock), and after an attempt fails at the receiver (a timeout, a broken connection, a status other than 2xx) a wait before the next (30 s, doubled per failure in a row, at most 10 minutes; a success ends it). An attempt that cannot go yet is put off without spending one of its 9: a new job runs it when the claim or the wait ends, at the earliest after 1 s doubled per put-off (at most 5 minutes) and up to 5 s of jitter, so a crowd never wakes at once (`deferrals` in the payload, counted on the delivery in the same transaction, so a job run twice never starts a second chain). A delivery older than two hours, or put off 1,000 times, spends its attempts instead, so it fails for good within about four hours. A crashed attempt's claim lapses after the timeout and 30 s. **At least once:** an attempt cut off after the receiver answered but before that was recorded is sent again, with the same `Idempotency-Key`: a receiver keeps the keys it handled and ignores one it has seen. Settled deliveries are kept `CCMS_WEBHOOKS_KEEP_DAYS` (default 30).
- **Where deliveries may go** (server-side request forgery): `https:` only (`http:` with `CCMS_WEBHOOKS_ALLOW_HTTP=true`, local testing only); no user name, password or fragment; never `localhost`, `*.local`, `*.internal` or an address that is not public unicast (private, carrier-grade NAT, loopback, link-local and so cloud metadata, documentation, multicast, reserved; IPv6 outside global unicast `2000::/3`, unique-local, link-local, local-use NAT64 and the other transition forms, whose embedded IPv4 address counts), checked when the URL is set and again on **every attempt after resolving the host**: when any address it resolves to is blocked the attempt fails (`blocked-address`), and the connection goes to the address checked (no second lookup, so DNS rebinding cannot redirect it); TLS still checks the certificate against the host name. `CCMS_WEBHOOKS_ALLOW_PRIVATE=true` lifts the address rule for local testing only. No connection is reused; each attempt is cut off after `CCMS_WEBHOOKS_TIMEOUT_MS` (default 10 s); at most 16 KiB of the answer's head is read and its body never. Logs name an outbox row, a delivery or a subscriber by ID and an error by its code: never a URL, a header, a body or a secret.
- **Verifying a delivery** (the recipe; `verifyWebhookSignature` in `core/webhook-signature.ts` is it, tested against real deliveries): `X-Ccms-Signature` is `t=<unix seconds>,v1=<hex>`, with a second `v1` during a rotation.
  1. Read the raw body as bytes, before parsing it.
  2. Split the header at `,`; take `t` and every `v1`.
  3. Refuse a `t` more than 5 minutes from your clock (a replay).
  4. Compute HMAC-SHA256 with the whole secret (`whsec_…`, as UTF-8) as the key over `<t>.<raw body>` (the timestamp in decimal, a full stop, the body), as lowercase hex.
  5. Compare it with each `v1` in constant time; accept when one matches. Then ignore an `Idempotency-Key` you have handled already.

  ```js
  import { createHmac, timingSafeEqual } from 'node:crypto';
  function verify(header, rawBody, secret, now = Date.now()) {
    const parts = header.split(',').map((p) => p.split('='));
    const t = Number(parts.find(([k]) => k === 't')?.[1]);
    if (!Number.isInteger(t) || Math.abs(now / 1000 - t) > 300) return false;
    const expected = createHmac('sha256', secret)
      .update(`${t}.`)
      .update(rawBody)
      .digest();
    return parts
      .filter(([k, v]) => k === 'v1' && /^[0-9a-f]{64}$/.test(v))
      .some(([, v]) => timingSafeEqual(Buffer.from(v, 'hex'), expected));
  }
  ```

- **Tables** (`repos/webhooks.tables.ts`, migrations `0026_webhooks`, `0029_webhooks_test_and_audience` (#254) and `0039_webhook_headers` (#457)): `webhooks` (`url`, `description`, `events`, `max_audience`, `headers` (jsonb, `[{ name, secret: false, value } | { name, secret: true, sealed }]`), `secret_sealed`, `previous_secret_sealed` and `previous_secret_until`, `secret_rotated_at`, `active`, `disabled_reason`, `disabled_at`, `consecutive_failures`, `attempt_failures`, `sending_until` and `next_attempt_at` (the attempt in flight and the wait after a failure), `owner_kind`, `owner_id`, `created_at`, `updated_at`) and `webhook_deliveries` (`webhook_id`, `event`, `idempotency_key`, `body`, `owner_kind` and `owner_id` (the owner the body was made for), `max_audience` (the audience limit it was built under), `status` (`pending`, `succeeded`, `failed`, `skipped`), `attempts`, `deferrals`, `last_attempt_at`, `last_status_code`, `last_error` (a code: `http-status`, `timeout`, `connection`, `blocked-address`, `blocked-url`, `secret-unreadable`), `sent_headers` (the names the last attempt sent), `delivered_at`, `outbox_id` (null for a test event, and only for one), `created_at`; deleted with its subscriber), the formats checked by the database too.
- **Tests** never call out: deliveries go to a receiver on `127.0.0.1` (`tests/webhooks.e2e.spec.ts`, with http and private addresses allowed for that app only), the sender's address checks run against a resolver the test provides, and the actions against fakes (`tests/fakes/fake-webhooks.ts`).

## Change feed

The `changes` component (`src/changes/`, #43): **`GET /changes?page[cursor]=&page[size]=`** (`content.read`, `ReadChangesAction`; delivery API) tells what was released, withdrawn or deprecated since a cursor, as the reader may read it, so a consumer that was down catches up and one that starts fresh gets a snapshot first. It carries the webhook events (the same shapes and idempotency keys, so a consumer can use either) and, per release, the retrieval chunks to store and to drop, so a retrieval index (a vector store) stays in sync.

- **After a restore** onto another database server (#619): while the outbox holds the feed ("Outbox"), every page is 503 `outbox-blocked` (`Retry-After: 60`; the reader keeps their cursor, nothing is lost); once the recipe ran, a cursor whose positions no longer stand on their rows (`ChangeFeedSource.holds`, one lookup by ID per page) is 410 `cursor-gone`: resync.
- **Events** (`Change`: `id` the event's idempotency key, `attributes` `event`, `occurredAt`, `data`): `manual.released`, `module.released`, `release.withdrawn`, `module.deprecated` and `manual.deprecated` (#558) exactly as webhooks send them (`WebhookEventBuilder`, judged by the reader's scope now); and per chunk of `GET /chunks` (`<lang>/<moduleId>`, an FAQ entry's `<lang>/<moduleId>/<blockId>`): `chunk.upserted` (`chunkId`, `moduleId`, `lang`, `blockId`, `contentHash` as `GET /chunks` gives it, `release` `{ releaseId, product, revision, tag, commit }`: read it at `?ref=<commit>`) and `chunk.removed` (the same IDs, `reason` and the release now standing, or null). Reasons: `deprecated` (deprecated in its source-language file with no replacement; one with a replacement stays), `withdrawn` (its release was withdrawn) and `gone` (deleted, or no longer the reader's to read: never told which).
- **Chunk events** are the difference between released states: the chunks the reader holds at the release that stood before a row and at the one that stands after it, the newest release of `main` (a maintenance line's release tells its webhook events but moves no state) not withdrawn by then (`ChangeFeedSource.standingAt`). A release tells each chunk new or changed in its text or in what an index filters on (title, type, audience, facets, keywords, manuals, conditions; not `meta.release`), and each gone; a withdrawal steps back to the release before it, so what it added is `removed` (`withdrawn`) and what it changed is `upserted` as it was. A deletion, a deprecation, a chunk hidden by a changed audience and a dropped manual come out the same way. Read through `ListChunksAction`'s kept chunk sets (`everyChunk`) at the release's commit (by ID, not by tag name, which a branch could share), as a reader of exactly the reader's `content.read` scopes (`readerOf`), and kept per release and scopes (`ReleasedChunkStates`, the last 8).
- **No event skipped or told twice**, whatever is released meanwhile: the rows come from the outbox (`release.published`, `release.withdrawn`, `module.deprecated`, `manual.deprecated`; partial index `outbox_change_feed`, migration `0040_change_feed`, widened to the last in `0048_manual_deprecated_events`) in `(tx_id, id)` order and only below the settled horizon, as `OutboxFeed` reads it, so no row can later appear before one already told. Within a row, the webhook events are told by key and the chunks by ID, and the cursor says where it stopped. A page looks at no more than 100 rows (`ROWS_PER_PAGE`), starts no more than 4 costly steps (`TRANSITIONS_PER_PAGE`: a release's or a withdrawal's row, whose webhook events compare releases, and a chunk transition, which compares whole chunk sets) and answers at most `page[size]` events (default 50, at most 200); a release that changed thousands of chunks takes as many pages. What a transition tells is worked out once and kept (`PLANS_KEPT`, 16, beside a row's webhook events), so a page costs a search in it, not a sort; one page at a time per reader (another meanwhile is 429 `rate-limited`, `Retry-After: 1`). **A target that cannot be read** as the reader (an invalid configuration at its commit, chunks too large to cut together, #684 `chunk-set-too-large`, or a commit gone from the repository) is logged: a release nothing of which was told yet is skipped (its transition tells nothing and the reader keeps the state they hold, so the next release is compared with the last one that could be read and no removal is lost); a release part of which was told before the reader's scope changed (#558), a withdrawal back to such a release, a scope change to one and a snapshot of one remove every chunk the reader may hold (`withdrawn` for a withdrawal, else `gone`: what was told of the release so far too), after which the reader holds nothing there (the cursor says so), and the next release that can be read is told in full. **What the reader holds is always read again:** when it cannot be (the commit of a release whose chunks they hold is gone from the repository after a history rewrite or a garbage collection, it no longer reads after an upgrade, or a release they were told part of no longer reads in the same scope), the chunks to remove cannot be named, and the page is **410 `cursor-gone`** (`source.parameter` `page[cursor]`): resync (below). No chunk outlives what the reader may read without them hearing of it. Cursors from before #558 have no "holds nothing" mark: they read on as before from a state that reads, and are `cursor-gone` at one that does not. A page looks at no more than 25 deprecation rows (`DEPRECATIONS_PER_PAGE`): each opens its commit. The one-page-at-a-time rule holds per server: several servers each let one through.
- **Scoped to the reader now:** their scope is judged on every page. When it differs from the one the cursor was issued for, the next events carry their chunks over at the same release: `chunk.removed` (`gone`) for those they may no longer read, `chunk.upserted` for those they may now, to that reader only. No event names a chunk they may not read now, except a removal of one they were told of before (worked out by today's chunking: should an upgrade change how chunks are cut, a removal may name an ID of their old scope they were not told under that name). A scope change in the middle of a release's chunks starts them again from every version the reader may hold (what they started from, and what was told of the target so far); a transition starts from at most two such pieces (`MAX_PIECES`), so once a release's or a scope change's chunks have been restarted after something was told, another change before they end is `cursor-expired` (a snapshot, which starts from none, takes one more). The webhook events of a row are judged by the scope of each page too: should the scope widen in the middle of a row's events, the newly visible ones sorting before where it stopped are not told (its chunk events still are).
- **The cursor** (`meta.page.cursor`, also in `links.next`, given on every page, also once caught up: `meta.upToDate`) is sealed under the instance secret key (`SecretSealer`, see "Secrets") for that reader (`change-feed-cursor:v1:<kind>:<id>`): opaque, unforgeable, and of no use to anyone else. It holds outbox positions, release IDs, chunk IDs and the digest of the reader's scopes, never the scopes (those are kept by digest in `change_feed_scopes`, `FeedScopes`, only ever added to), so it stays under 2 KiB (a test holds it with the longest IDs, in the middle of a scope change). It holds no time: it never expires on its own, and survives restarts. It also holds a lineage (made at a fresh start) and a count of its transitions, which are part of every chunk event's key, so two events never share one, not even a scope changed back and forth at one release; and `CHUNK_FORMAT` (how chunks are cut and compared: raise it when an upgrade changes either, and older cursors are `cursor-expired`). **400 `cursor-expired`** when it cannot be read: the instance key was rotated (#489; the old key is gone, so an old cursor cannot be opened), it was issued to someone else, it was changed, it names scopes no longer kept, or it is of another `CHUNK_FORMAT`; start again without one. A malformed one is 400 `invalid-parameter`, any other parameter (`page[number]` too) 400 `unsupported-parameter`. A deactivated or deleted reader's requests are refused before the route (403 `user-deactivated`, 401 or 403 `unknown-user` by the guard), so their cursor reads nothing; one reactivated reads on from it, judged by their scope then. A reader who lost `content.read` gets 403 `forbidden`. Without an instance key the route is 404 `not-configured`, as webhooks are. `Cache-Control: private, no-store`.
- **A fresh start** (no cursor): the newest settled row is where it starts, with a `chunk.upserted` for every chunk the reader may read at the release standing there (none before the first release), then the events after it.
- **Resync** (after `cursor-gone`, or `cursor-expired`): either **from the start**: drop every chunk stored from the feed and read again without a cursor (a snapshot of every chunk you may read at the release standing now, then the changes); or **from a release tag**, to keep what has not changed: pick a release (`GET /releases`, its `tag`), page through `GET /chunks?ref=<tag>` ("Ingest recipe" under "Chunks": store each by `id`, embedding only a `contentHash` you do not have), drop every stored chunk that listing did not name, then read the feed again without a cursor; its snapshot upserts what changed since that release and, with the same `contentHash`, costs no embedding for the rest.
- Not here: drafts, reviews and imports.

### Integrating with a retrieval (RAG) pipeline

A minimal sync loop, in pseudo-code. Webhooks (`module.released`, `release.withdrawn`, …) only wake it up; the feed says what to do, so a missed delivery loses nothing.

```text
cursor := load()                          # null on the first run
loop:
  page := GET /changes?page[size]=200 (&page[cursor]=cursor when set)
  if page is 400 cursor-expired           # key rotated, or the scope store lost
     or page is 410 cursor-gone:          # what you hold is gone from the repository
    drop every stored chunk; cursor := null; continue   # the snapshot follows
                                          # (or resync from a release tag: "Resync")
  for event in page.data:
    d := event.attributes.data
    switch event.attributes.event:
      chunk.upserted:
        chunk := the one with id d.chunkId in
                 GET /chunks?ref={d.release.commit}&filter[module]={d.moduleId}&filter[lang]={d.lang}
        if store.hash(d.chunkId) != d.contentHash:
          store.upsert(d.chunkId, embed(chunk.text), chunk.attributes)
        else:
          store.updateMetadata(d.chunkId, chunk.attributes)  # only what it is filtered on changed
      chunk.removed:
        store.delete(d.chunkId)           # also when it is not there
      other:                              # webhook events: notify, log
  cursor := page.meta.page.cursor
  save(cursor)                            # with what was stored, in one step where you can
  if page.meta.upToDate: wait for a webhook or a minute
```

Applying an event twice does nothing more (an upsert of the same hash, a delete of what is gone), so save the cursor after the page is stored: a crash in between tells the page again.

## Jobs

The `jobs` component (`src/jobs/`, #41): slow work (release outputs #249, imports #256, webhook deliveries #42, translation jobs) runs in the background on [pg-boss](https://github.com/timgit/pg-boss) in the app's own database (no Redis), and survives restarts.

- **A kind** (`defineJob`, `core/job.ts`), defined in the component whose work it is: a name (`release.outputs`), a Zod schema for its payload, `retryLimit` (default 3 retries), `retryDelaySeconds` (default 10, doubled per retry with jitter, at most an hour), `timeoutSeconds` per attempt (default 15 min, at most a day less a minute) and `concurrency` per server (default 1; a handler's own `concurrency`, #497, overrides it, for a setting read at start) and `retentionSeconds`, how long a job may wait before the queue drops it without a `failed` call (default 14 days, pg-boss's; #497). **A handler** (`JobHandler`, a provider in that component's module; the jobs component finds every one among the app's providers at start-up): `run(payload, { id, attempt, lastAttempt, signal })`, and optionally `failed(payload)`, called once a job has failed for good (its last attempt threw, timed out or its server vanished), so the record it worked on never waits forever (itself retried, up to 10 times).
- **Enqueueing** (`JobQueue`, `core/job-queue.ts`): `enqueue(kind, payload, { key, tx?, startAfter?, priority? })`; `priority` (#497, a whole number from 0 to `MAX_JOB_PRIORITY`, 2^31 − 1; default 0) orders a queue's waiting jobs, highest first, then oldest first, and `prioritize(kind, key, priority)` raises the waiting job of that kind and key to it (never lowers; `false` when none waits). `key` names the work (an output row, an import): at most one job per kind and key is queued or running (pg-boss's `exclusive` policy), so the same work enqueued twice (a retried request, two servers) is one job; `undefined` says one was there, queued or already running, so a change made after a running attempt read its state is not picked up by it: a producer that needs that enqueues again once the job has ended, and `failed` may run after a new job for the key exists. Both re-check by compare-and-swap. With `tx` the job is written in the caller's transaction (pg-boss's Drizzle adapter on `drizzleOf(tx)`): it exists exactly when the rows describing its work do. `find(kind, id)`, `latest(kind, key)` (the newest job of that kind and key, ended or not; `undefined` once cleaned up), `openKeys(kind, keys)` (#497, #527: which of those keys have a job queued, retrying or running; one query, however many keys) and `cancel(kind, id)` for the component that owns the work. **No routes for jobs** (but `GET /jobs/status`, below, for administrators): a component shows its jobs' progress in its own resources, behind its own permissions, and decides who may cancel.
- **Payloads are data, not trust.** At most 16 KiB of JSON: IDs, never content or secrets. Checked against the kind's schema on enqueue and again before every run; a stored payload that does not fit is never handed to the handler (the job is cancelled and logged). A handler reads what the IDs name again and acts as its component's service actor, never as whoever caused the job (the one exception: an import is written as its uploader, resolved again by ID, so its change set and every check are theirs; see "Imports").
- **At least once.** An attempt cut off by a crash, a shutdown or a timeout runs again, and its own effects may have happened already, so a handler is idempotent: it reads the current state first and moves it by compare-and-swap (an output row from `queued` to `done`). Every settlement (complete, fail, heartbeat) names the attempt it was claimed as (`{ id, retryCount }`), so a late attempt never overwrites a newer one.
- **The worker** (`JobWorker`, `api/job-worker.ts`) claims from each queue every `CCMS_JOBS_POLL_MS` (default 2 s) and again as soon as an attempt ends, runs each through `RunJobAction` and settles it; a failure is kept as the error's name and message only (at most 1,000 characters). A running attempt sends a heartbeat every 10 s; one whose claim lapsed (cancelled, or handed to another server) is aborted (`signal`). One past its `timeoutSeconds` is failed, retried and aborted. **A server that dies** leaves its attempts to the heartbeat: pg-boss's maintenance retries them 30 s after the last beat. **Shutdown** claims nothing more, waits up to 5 s for running attempts (before the HTTP server and the database close), then hands the rest back to the queue (fenced to the attempt; it does not count, so a deploy never uses up a job's retries) and aborts them; a claim in flight hands its jobs straight back. `CCMS_JOBS_POLL_MS=0`: this server runs no jobs and only enqueues (tests; a deployment that leaves the work to dedicated servers). Servers side by side share the queues.
- **Where:** pg-boss's tables are in the schema `pgboss`, beside the app's and outside Drizzle's migrations. It installs and upgrades them itself at start (its own advisory lock) unless `CCMS_DATABASE_MIGRATE=false`; then it only checks their version and `db-migrate` installs them. It uses a pool of its own (`CCMS_JOBS_POOL_MAX`, default 4), so polling never waits behind requests for a connection, nor requests behind it. Recurring jobs (`schedule`) are off until a component needs one.
- **Tests (the seam):** pg-boss runs on PGlite through its own adapter (`fromPglite`), so the tests run the queue's real SQL: the test template (`tests/database.ts`) has its schema installed, `createTestApp` hands it the test database (`JobsConnection`) with maintenance off and `CCMS_JOBS_POLL_MS=0`, and a test runs what is due synchronously with `t.runJobs()` (`JobWorker.drain`: the same claiming and settling as the timer), or `boss.supervise()` for maintenance. PGlite has one connection, so two servers claiming the same queue at once (SKIP LOCKED) is not tested. Unit tests of producers and handlers use `FakeJobQueue` (`tests/fakes/`).
- Image renditions ("Media library") are made in the request, with a concurrency slot; moving them onto jobs later is possible, not done.

### Roles (`CCMS_ROLE`, #527)

One image, one `AppModule`, and `CCMS_ROLE` (`src/config/role.ts`, the one list of roles; default `all`) decides what a process starts:

| Role     | HTTP                                                                      | Jobs                 | Background loops | PDF pool                                  | Migrations     |
| -------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------- | ---------------- | ----------------------------------------- | -------------- |
| `all`    | the API                                                                   | consumes every queue | yes              | `CCMS_PDF_WORKERS` (one per spare core)   | applies them   |
| `api`    | the API                                                                   | enqueues only        | none             | at most 2 by default, for readers' PDFs   | applies them   |
| `worker` | `/health` and `/health/ready` only (see Health)                           | consumes every queue | yes              | `CCMS_PDF_WORKERS`, all for release files | waits for them |
| `backup` | none: `main.ts` runs the backup service (#529), its arguments the command | –                    | –                | –                                         | –              |

The background loops are the release-output sweep, the change-set reconciler, the import sweeper, the notifications and webhooks outbox consumers, the storage recount and the search embedding's catch-up at start (`search.embed`): each asks `ServerRole.runsBackground` before it schedules anything, and pg-boss supervises (its maintenance) only where jobs are consumed. A worker is `NestFactory.createApplicationContext(AppModule)`: the same providers, no HTTP adapter, so no controller, guard or body parser is ever reachable on it. It lowers its CPU priority first (`CCMS_WORKER_NICE`, default 10; only ever raised; on Linux for each of its threads), which the PDF processes it forks inherit, so on a host shared with the API its renders yield the CPU to readers (compose can also limit each worker's CPUs: `OPENFOLIA_WORKER_CPUS`). Interactive renders (a manual's PDF, page images, checks) stay in the API's own small pool (lazy: its processes start with the first PDF), bounded by `CCMS_PDF_QUEUE` (4 per PDF worker) and answered 503 `renderer-busy` with `Retry-After` past it; never through the job queue (#527: a worker's PDF processes are busy with release files, and a render cannot be interrupted, so a preview there would wait for one to end; and previews would depend on a worker being up. Measured on `large` with a backlog rendering, on a busy machine: a preview took a median 1.1 s with the API and one worker apart against 6.8 s in one `all` process, a manual's PDF 1.4 s against 30 s); a release's files render on the workers (`CCMS_RENDER_CONCURRENCY` defaults to all of a worker's PDF workers, half in `all`).

**Consumers.** Each consuming process (`all`, `worker`) records itself in `job_consumers` (`JobConsumers`, `repos/drizzle-job-consumers.ts`) every 15 s: its host, PID, start and whether it claimed or ran an attempt since its last beat (`consumed_at`); it removes its row on a clean shutdown, and rows quiet for a day are removed by the next beat. Times are the database's. `GET /jobs/status` (`admin.roles`; one `JobsStatus` resource, id `jobs`) reads it with pg-boss's table: `consumers` (heard from in the last minute), `lastConsumedAt`, `ready` (due and waiting), `running`, `oldestReadyAt`, and in `meta` `lagSeconds`, `level` (`warning` when jobs waited 5 minutes, `stallSeconds`, and none was consumed for as long: nothing takes them) and `checkedAt`. The editor's System page shows it ("Background work"). This is the one route about jobs; a component still shows its jobs' progress in its own resources.

**What one process kept, and where it is now** (the single-process inventory, #527). Every lock, cap, cursor and slot held in memory, and whether it holds with several processes (more workers, and API processes beside them):

- **In Postgres, so shared by every process:**
  - Jobs are claimed once (pg-boss's `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`; a lapsed claim is settled by attempt), oldest first within a priority, whichever process polls.
  - The release-output sweep's cursor (`release_output_sweeps`, one row, moved by compare-and-swap on its version), so two workers' sweeps share one window and never repeat it.
  - The per-release caps (`QUEUED_PER_RELEASE`, `PROMOTED_PER_RELEASE`), under the release's advisory lock (`SCHEDULE_LOCK`, transaction-scoped), whichever API or worker schedules or moves up.
  - **Planning order** (#497's first-in-first-out per server; strict since #527): a product's releases are planned one at a time across every process, and strictly in the order they were cut. The turn: `PlanningTurns` (`repos/database-planning-turns.ts`: `Database.withSessionLock`, the session advisory lock (`PLANNING_LOCK` 527, `hashtext(product)`) on a connection of its own outside the pool, waited for in Postgres's lock queue, so the job that asked first gets the turn next, the wait cancelled when the attempt's signal aborts). The order: in the turn, the planning first plans the product's earlier releases still to be planned (`earlierToPlan`: not planned to the end in Postgres, `release_plannings`, and a plan job not ended in pg-boss's table, both read inside the turn; at most `EARLIER_PLANNING_MS` of an attempt), oldest first, then its own release. That is the ticket per product: the release's place in its product's cut order (`publishedAt`, then ID), read from Postgres, never a process's queue. A release reads the one cut before it to share its files; planned before that one, it would render again what it could have shared (#497's `joinLater` then recovers only the files not rendered yet). Plan jobs are claimed newest first across products, so whichever of a product's jobs a worker claims, the turn plans the product's releases from the oldest still to be planned up to it; a worker waiting for the turn holds a claimed job, never one that an earlier release's planning needs, so nothing waits in a circle. A lost connection loses the lock and the planning goes on: another may plan beside it, which costs renders, never correctness (each planning is idempotent). Waiting holds the worker's planning slot (`concurrency` 1) and counts against the attempt's 15 minutes; the earlier releases' share, `EARLIER_PLANNING_MS`, counts from the attempt's start, waiting included, so a turn taken late plans its own release at once. Since the first holder plans the backlog and those after it find their releases planned (`release_plannings`), a waiter waits about one share plus a few plannings; a job can still run out of tries behind a backlog longer than its attempts (then its release, if not planned by another's turn, waits for a publisher's "plan again").
  - Notifications, webhooks, imports and the recount: by claim or compare-and-swap on their rows; migrations under their advisory lock; the instance secret key, created exclusively (`link`), checked under its lock.
- **Per process, by design** (each bounds what one process spends, memory or CPU; with more processes, each has its own, and the bound for the whole is that many times one):
  - Render concurrency (`CCMS_RENDER_CONCURRENCY`), the PDF pool and its queue (`CCMS_PDF_WORKERS`, `CCMS_PDF_QUEUE`).
  - The `OneAtATime` gates (#720): the checks' renders (`renderer-busy`) and the chunk sets a check builds for its verdict (`chunk-sets-busy`, `CHUNK_SET_BUILDS_WAITING`). They exist to bound one process's memory (a whole commit's chunk set is built in it), so they stay per process.
  - The imports' busy slot (`MAX_CONCURRENT_IMPORTS`), the style review's rate, image renditions' slots, the search's per-reader rate and searches at once (#539), the package sends (`PACKAGE_SENDS_AT_ONCE`, per reader), the failed-authentication windows (`CCMS_AUTH_FAILURES_MAX`; password and code attempts are counted in Postgres). With several API processes behind one address, the failed-authentication limit is per process: route a client to one process (sticky) or rate-limit at the proxy.
  - The change feed's page lock (#43, `ReadChangesAction`: one page under way per reader, a `Set` in the process; checked again for #527): a cost bound, safe per process. What a page answers does not depend on it: it is read from the sealed cursor and the outbox, and the only thing a page writes is the scopes behind a digest (`FeedScopes.save`, content-addressed and only added to, so two writes of one digest keep one row), so the same cursor read twice, in one process or two, at once or one after the other, gives the same page and leaves the same rows. With several API processes a reader may have one page under way in each: at most that many times one page's cost, which the per-page bounds (`ROWS_PER_PAGE`, `TRANSITIONS_PER_PAGE`, `DEPRECATIONS_PER_PAGE`) keep small.
  - The notifications' and webhooks' fan-out attempts per outbox row (`MAX_ATTEMPTS`): a row failing on two workers is tried up to that many times on each before it is passed over.
  - The reconciler's resume hint and its "missing" log dedupe, the search sweep's random starting point (a hint and a log line, nothing decided by them).
- **Caches** keyed by a commit or by content (PDFs and page images, chunk sets, module indexes, histories, diffs, release states of the change feed): what they hold never changes, so any number of processes may each keep their own.
- **Held by the API process that made it:** a validated translation import (`MemoryTranslationImportStore`, an hour), applied by the same process from the import screen. One API process (as in compose) is fine; several API processes behind one address need sticky sessions for it. Nothing a job makes is held in a process's memory: the seven job kinds (`release.plan-outputs`, `release.outputs`, `release.offline-package`, `import.apply`, `mail.send`, `webhook.deliver`, `search.embed`) write their results to Postgres and the blob store, so the API reads them whichever worker ran them.
- **Fixed, not a process matter:** `ListChunksAction.everyChunk` (the change feed's chunk sets, `ReleasedChunkStates`) was called without the action's policy. It now holds it as `handle` does (#527): it takes the actor and authorizes `content.read`, and refuses (403, before any chunk is cut) a snapshot not opened for that actor (another reading key, `content.read.draft`, an overlay), so the set it gives is exactly what `GET /chunks` would give that reader. The feed passes the reader it opened the snapshot as (`readerOf` the caller's `content.read` scopes; a cursor's kept scopes are the caller's own when it was sealed), behind `ReadChangesAction`'s own `content.read`.

**The readers' p95 beside a backlog** (#527's criterion) is measured on a GitHub-hosted Linux runner, a quiet machine of its own: the `perf-backlog` workflow (`.github/workflows/perf-backlog.yml`, run by hand only) seeds the `large` profile and runs `api:perf-scale`'s `backlog` rows (`all` against `api` beside one worker, each with nothing rendering and with the seed's releases planning and rendering); the report is the run's summary and an artifact (`scripts/perf/README.md`).

Two workers rendering a backlog are checked by `tests/two-workers.postgres.spec.ts` (the built server in four processes on one Postgres, two `api` and two `worker`: two releases of one product, every output done after at most one attempt, each file rendered once for both, at most one render per worker at once, at most `PROMOTED_PER_RELEASE` moved up although both APIs are asked for every file; it runs when `CCMS_TEST_PG_URL` names a server, after `pnpm nx build api`), by `src/database/session-lock.postgres.spec.ts` (the turns across two pools), by the compose smoke test, and by `api:perf-scale`'s `backlog` row.

## Imports

The `imports` component (`src/imports/`, #256): content from elsewhere comes in as a bundle of the documented import format (`@openfolia/content-model`, "Import format"), is checked against the target branch, and is then written into a new change set, reviewed like any other.

- **Importers** (`Importer`, `core/importer.ts`): a port with an `id`, a `label`, a `description`, an options schema (Zod) and `toBundle(file, options, limits, { config })`, which turns an upload into a bundle (its files by path), for the target branch's configuration (`openfolia.yaml`; a retried job converts again with the change set branch's, so the same file gives the same items). Two ship: `bundle` (`BundleImporter`), a ZIP of the format itself, and `dita` (`DitaImporter`, #432), a ZIP of a DITA 1.3 project converted by `@openfolia/dita` with the configuration's `dita` mapping (below); neither takes options. A converter from another system is one more provider in `IMPORTERS` (`imports.module.ts`); none is tied to a vendor. `ImporterRegistry` lists them by ID. `GET /importers` (paged) shows each with its options as JSON Schema (`meta.optionsSchema`, input side) and the largest upload (`meta.maxBytes`, `CCMS_IMPORT_MAX_BYTES`), for a screen to render and to state.
- **Who:** every route needs `admin.roles`; checking and applying need `content.edit` too (in some role), and each item is then checked against the actor's scope exactly as saving it would be (`checkSubject`, `checkChange`): one outside it is refused (`outside-scope`), never written. An import is its uploader's: anyone else's is 404 like a missing one.
- **`POST /imports`** (`CreateImportAction`): `multipart/form-data` with `X-Ccms-Request`, the file in the part `file` (at most `CCMS_IMPORT_MAX_BYTES`, read into memory) and the text fields `importer` (default `bundle`), `options` (a JSON object), `updateExisting`, `dryRun` (`true`/`false`, default `false`) and `baseRef` (default the server's default branch; never a change set's). At most `MAX_CONCURRENT_IMPORTS` (2) are checked at once per server (503 `import-busy`, checked before the form is read). The importer makes the bundle, the validator checks it against the configuration at `baseRef`, the module and manual IDs there (as the actor reads them) and the files already stored that the importer may read (`maxDepth` `CCMS_IMPORT_MAX_JSON_DEPTH`; an `import.json` over `CCMS_IMPORT_MAX_DOCUMENT_BYTES` is refused before it is parsed, `document-too-large`, one with more than `MAX_IMPORT_VALUES` (1,000,000) values, `too-many-values`, or more than `MAX_IMPORT_ITEMS` (2,000) modules and manuals before any is checked, `too-many-items`; the validator also refuses a module or manual of more than 50,000 values, any more once malformed ones hold 100,000 values in all (`not-checked`), and a converter report of more than 10,000 entries unchecked, and keeps 100 findings per item: so a small upload's work and report stay bounded), and `planImport` decides each item:
  - **Files already stored** (#332, `ReadableAssets.storedFor`, `assets/core/readable-assets.ts`, the rule working copies use too, #335): a module may refer to a file it does not carry only when the importer may read it already: a module they may see at `baseRef` uses it (`ReadableContent.assetUsage()`), or it was uploaded into a change set they are the author of (`AssetUploads.uploadedForAuthor`, the scope of the author's preview); and the blob store holds it. Any other reference (another author's upload, an import's uploaded bundle, a file only content outside their scope uses, bytes nobody stored) is `missing-asset`, the same finding whatever the store holds, and the store is not asked for it, so the report is no way to learn whether a file exists. The job checks again with the same rule.
  - `create`; `update` an existing module (per language) or manual, only with `updateExisting`; `skip` one that exists without it (`exists`, the default from #242), one the target holds exactly so even with it (`unchanged`: the same file once printed, so importing the same source again writes nothing, #432) and a file no written module uses (`unused`); `refuse` one with a validation error, outside the scope, larger than a working copy may be (`too-large`), or a file the upload route would refuse (`unsupported-image-type`, `unsupported-attachment-type`, `too-large`: the asset rules, by the bytes).
  - The report (`meta.report`): `valid`, `applicable` (valid, nothing refused, within one change set's 1,000 locks and 1,000 uploads, something to write), `applicableWithoutRefused` (#758: `applicable`, or the rest stands without the refused items; below), the bundle's issues (at most 200), the items in document order then the files (at most 20 reasons and 20 other findings each, `issueCount` all), the converter's own report passed through (at most 1,000 entries), and the counts (`create`, `update`, `skip`, `refuse`, `passthrough` blocks, errors, warnings, …).
  - **A dry run** (`dryRun=true`) answers 200 and stores nothing: no blob, no row, no change set, no Git object. Otherwise the upload goes into the blob store (`application/octet-stream`, never served) and the import with its report into `imports` (201, `Location`), `validated`, for an hour (`IMPORT_TTL_MS`).
- **ZIP safety** (`readZip`, `core/zip-archive.ts`; nothing is ever written to disk, paths are only map keys): the central directory is read and checked whole before a byte is inflated. Refused as 422 `invalid-archive` with `meta.reason`: a path that is absolute, holds `..` or `.` or an empty segment, a backslash, a colon, a control character, is longer than 1,024 bytes or not UTF-8 (`unsafe-path`); deeper than `CCMS_IMPORT_MAX_PATH_DEPTH` folders (`path-too-deep`); the same path twice, or a path both a file and a folder (`duplicate-path`); a symbolic link or another special file (`link`); more than `CCMS_IMPORT_MAX_FILES` entries (`too-many-files`); more than `CCMS_IMPORT_MAX_UNPACKED_BYTES` declared unpacked (`too-large`); more than 1 MiB declared unpacked and more than 100 times its packed size, a zip bomb (`too-compressed`, `meta.max` the ratio); encryption, ZIP64, several disks, a method other than stored or deflate; entries whose data overlap each other or the directory, a local header that disagrees with the directory, a file that inflates past its declared size (inflating stops there) or fails its CRC-32 (`corrupt`); anything that is no ZIP (`not-a-zip`). An archive inside is a file like any other, never opened. Only the DITA importer parses XML, with the strict reader (`@openfolia/xml`: no DTD read or fetched, no entity expanded, bounded) and bounded content-reference and key expansion, elements and characters both (`docs/dita-import.md`); the conversion runs in a child process whose heap and time are bounded (`CCMS_IMPORT_CONVERT_MAX_MEMORY_MB`, `CCMS_IMPORT_CONVERT_TIMEOUT_MS`), and one past either, or past what a string may hold, is a 422 `import-too-large` with `meta.reason` `memory`, `timeout` or `size`.
  - **The rest without the refused items** (`planWithRest`, `core/import-rest.ts`, #758): when something is refused, nothing about the bundle as a whole is an error and something is written, the bundle without the refused items (their entries taken out of `import.json` by their pointers, their files out of the archive; for every importer, since it is the bundle the importer made) is validated and planned again in full. `applicableWithoutRefused` holds only when that second check is applicable and decides every other item exactly as the first, in the same order: a manual naming a refused module, a module showing or referring to a refused one, or a module using a refused file then reads as a dangling reference or a missing file (unless the target holds it already), decided differently, so it is false. The second check runs within the same `MAX_CONCURRENT_IMPORTS` slot and doubles the work only for a bundle with refused items.
- **`POST /imports/:importId/apply`** (`ApplyImportAction`, 202): only a `validated`, applicable, unexpired import of the caller's (409 `invalid-state`, `import-expired`; 422 `import-not-applicable`); `applying`, with its job (`import.apply`) queued in the same transaction. The body is optional: `{ "meta": { "skipRefused": true } }` (`ApplyImportDocument`) applies an import whose report refuses items without them, only when `applicableWithoutRefused` holds (else 422 `import-not-applicable`, `meta.skipRefused: false`); without it, an import with a refused item is refused as before (`meta.skipRefused` says whether the flag would help). Nothing else changes: the same expiry, state and concurrency rules, and the job writes only what the report decided `create` or `update`. Nothing records the flag: an import applying with refused items in its report was applied with it (the apply takes no other).
- **The job** (`RunImportAction`, `api/import-job.handler.ts`) acts as the uploader, resolved again by ID (one who can no longer act fails it: `importer-gone`; one who no longer holds `admin.roles` and `content.edit`: `forbidden`), through the change-set and asset actions an editor's requests use, with all their checks: it checks the import again against `baseRef` as it is then (other decisions, or no longer applicable: failed `import-outdated`; an import with refused items must still have `applicableWithoutRefused`, and refuse the same items), creates the change set (`Import of <file name>`; with refused items left out, its description and every commit message say so, `leftOutText`: "Left out: N items the check refused." and up to 25 of them, each with its reasons' codes and the first reason, one line of at most 300 characters each), uploads the files (`UploadAssetAction`, image or attachment as the modules use them), takes each module's and manual's lock and saves its working copy, source language first, then the translations, then the manuals, and makes a save point (`CommitWorkingCopiesAction`) every `CCMS_IMPORT_COMMIT_FILES` files or `CCMS_IMPORT_COMMIT_BYTES` of working copies (at most a save point's own 1,000 files and 32 MiB), so a large import is several commits on one change set. Progress is kept on the import (`meta.progress`: phase, files uploaded, modules and manuals committed, the commits); a retried attempt replays the stored decisions from the same bundle and resumes after the save points made (a working copy saved already is saved again at its version). A domain error fails it for good (`meta.failure`, e.g. `locked` when another change set holds a module); a passing one (the database, the blob store, a moved branch) is retried by the queue; one failing for good marks it failed (`import-failed`). Its end is one `import.apply` audit event on `import/<id>` with the counts (`refuse` among them: what was left out), what was written and the number of commits; never content.
- **`GET /imports/:importId`**: the import with its report, state, progress, failure and its change set (`relationships.changeSet`).
- **Clean-up** (`ImportSweeper`, `SweepImportsAction`): 90 s after start-up, then every ten minutes, imports past `expiresAt` (an hour after the upload, or after the job's end) that are not applying are removed with the bytes only they stored, under the hash's advisory lock asset uploads take (`AssetUploads.lockHash`): bytes an image or attachment upload records, or another import names, stay (that import takes the duty over), and so do bytes content refers to anywhere (`AssetReferences`, as for deleting an unused file, #332): the bytes are first marked deleted under the lock (a working copy naming them is refused meanwhile, 409 `asset-deleted`), then where they are used is read, then, under the lock again and with everything decided again, the import is removed and the bytes deleted or kept, and the mark cleared (a mark set by a deletion of the same bytes stays). A failure leaves the import and its bytes for the next sweep. While an import is kept, the media library counts its bundle as used (`HeldBlobs`, below): never listed as unused, never deleted.
- **Table** `imports` (`repos/imports.tables.ts`, migration `0022_imports`): the import with its options, base, creator, upload (hash, size, whether it stored the bytes), state, report and progress (JSON), failure, change set and times; formats checked by the database, `failure` set exactly when failed.
- **Settings:** `CCMS_IMPORT_MAX_BYTES` (64 MiB), `CCMS_IMPORT_MAX_FILES` (5,000), `CCMS_IMPORT_MAX_UNPACKED_BYTES` (256 MiB), `CCMS_IMPORT_MAX_PATH_DEPTH` (4), `CCMS_IMPORT_MAX_JSON_DEPTH` (128), `CCMS_IMPORT_MAX_DOCUMENT_BYTES` (8 MiB, at most 32 MiB), `CCMS_IMPORT_COMMIT_FILES` (1,000), `CCMS_IMPORT_COMMIT_BYTES` (32 MiB); see `.env.example`.
- **The DITA importer** (`dita`, #432; `@openfolia/dita`, `docs/dita-import.md` for the mapping table): the archive's root maps (in the source language) become manuals, their DITAVAL files (`<ditavalref>`) variants, their top-level topics modules (IDs from their paths, so a second import of the same project is `unchanged` and a changed topic an `update`), translations from top-level folders named as configured languages, images files. Content references, keys and profiling are resolved; what has no counterpart is in the bundle's converter report (`meta.report.report`, each message starting with a stable `[code]`), never dropped silently. The mapping is `dita` in `openfolia.yaml` (`@openfolia/content-model`, "DITA mapping"); `GET /config` checks it (`meta.ditaIssues`).
- **Connectors** (#141, `connectors/`): optional tools outside the API that produce the import format: a converter of a generic wiki markup (a one-off migration) and a sample issue-tracker connector that runs on a schedule, or on a signed trigger from the source (`--listen`, its README), through these routes (`POST /imports` dry run, then upload, apply, `GET /locks` first so it never writes what an open change set holds, and `POST /change-sets/:id/submit`), acting with a person's session. The API never imports one (lint, `eslint.config.mjs`); `tests/import-connectors.e2e.spec.ts` runs the tracker connector against the real API.
- **The screen** (#93): the editor's Admin page, "Import" (`frontend/editor/src/app/admin/import/`), drives these routes: the importer, the upload (checked against `meta.maxBytes` before it is sent), the options form from `meta.optionsSchema`, the dry run (stored, so the report survives a reload and Import applies the very upload checked), apply and its progress. A report with refused items whose rest stands (`applicableWithoutRefused`) offers to import the rest, for every importer, applied with `skipRefused` once confirmed (#758).
- The DITA export (#433) is its own component, "DITA export" below.

## Checks

The `checks` component (`src/checks/`, #71): before review or release, authors see what holds it up and where to fix it. Derived on every request from the content and the blob store, never stored.

- **`GET /manuals/:id/checks`** (`content.read`, `?ref=` like every content route): the manifest's validation issues; every published variant resolved in every configured language the reader may read the manual in (`ReadableContent.resolve`: missing modules and translations, text and variant attributes a variant leaves unset, cross-references, module reference cycles, modules above the manual's audience); and the checks of every module it includes, also through module references, in each of those languages (the module in that language, else its source-language file, as resolving falls back): `validateModule`, module references to nothing, images and attached files neither the blob store nor the repository's old `assets/` layout holds (`missing-image`, `missing-attachment`), and term links (#245) to a term that does not exist (`unknown-term`) or is deprecated (`deprecated-term`). A broken manifest is one `invalid-manifest` check (for a reader who may learn of it); a manual the reader may not see is 404, like a missing one. `@BeyondTheCommit()`: the answer depends on the blob store too, so it is never `immutable`.
- **`GET /change-sets/:changeSetId/checks`** (`content.read`; the change set found as `GET /change-sets/:changeSetId` finds it, its sides opened as for its changes): the subjects its changes list names, at its head (the review head once submitted): each changed module there (as above, plus its cross-references, a `missing-translation` per target language the reader would see a translation in for a source-language module (and an `outdated-translation` on each such translation there is whose source blocks changed since, see "Translations"), and a `module-cycle` at each of its module reference blocks whose references lead back to it, #224), each changed manual there (as the manual route), and, for a module gone from the head, where it is still used there: a `missing-module` on each manual naming it and on each module reference to it, an `xref-unknown-module` on each cross-reference to it (nothing else about those modules: their other problems are not the change set's).
  - **Cycles a change set closes** are found from each changed module, whether or not a manual includes it: its module references are followed (in its language, else the source language's file, as resolving reads them; whatever their variant conditions, since a cycle in any variant is one) and each reference block from which they lead back to it gets one `module-cycle` (`params.module`: the module it refers to, unless itself; the message names the shortest way round, `b → a → b`). A changed manual reports the cycles resolving finds in it as before; both name the same check when they meet at the same block. **A hidden module in a cycle:** references are followed only through modules the reader may see (and whose file can be read), so a cycle through a module the reader may not see is not reported to them at all, neither named nor counted, exactly as resolving does for a manual; a reader who sees every module in it gets it (`checks.actions.spec.ts`). **Bounded:** one search per changed module (what it reaches, then back to which of those lead to it), each module's references read once per request, and at most 100,000 references followed per request (`CYCLE_STEPS`); past that, only the cycles already found (and references to a module itself) are reported in that request; a message names at most 20 modules of the way round, then `…`. Cycles are looked for in the changed module's own language only: one that exists only through another language's files (a target-language manual falling back to this source-language module) shows on that manual's checks.
- **Retrieval chunks** (#688, #710, #711): each module checked gets an `expansion-too-large` when its chunks, cut as `GET /chunks` cuts them over what the reader may see, pass the limits of one module ("Retrieval chunks"). The cuts of one checks request (a manual's, or a change set's over the modules it changes) are made once everything else is checked, in module ID, then language order, all on one budget: `ExpansionBudget.forContent` over the modules checked, four times their content plus room for 32 modules at the limits of one, as `GET /chunks` holds a commit's, so a manual of a hundred small modules each including a lot costs at most about 32 modules' work, never a hundred. Past it, the remaining modules are not cut and one `chunks-not-checked` warning at the first of them says how many module files, a module in one language each (`params.modules`). Each cut is kept with what it spent (`CHUNK_CUTS_KEPT` per commit, reading key, language and module), and a kept cut is spent from the budget all the same, so checking again does no work and says the same; a cut is kept only while the budget has room for a whole module at the limits (else, and through an overlay, it is cut on the request's budget itself and not kept). Counted, never timed. **A change set's head as a whole** (#710): its checks also cut the chunks of the whole head commit as the reader sees it, as `GET /chunks` would once released (`ListChunksAction.tooLarge`, alongside the other checks: from the kept sets when there is one; what it finds is kept only as a verdict, `CHUNK_SET_VERDICTS_KEPT`, 64, never among the kept sets, so checking heads never pushes out the sets readers are served from, and `GET /chunks` answers a refusal kept there without work; content read through an overlay is kept nowhere). Such sets are built one at a time across requests, at most `CHUNK_SET_BUILDS_WAITING` (4) waiting; a checks request past that is 503 `chunk-sets-busy` with `Retry-After`, so many heads checked at once never build many whole commits together. That is the cost of a cold `GET /chunks` of the head, once per head commit and reading key: what a change set changes cannot tell whether the whole set fits, as one module's change changes what every module including it expands to. Past the set's budget, a blocking `chunk-set-too-large` at each module of the refusal's `meta.largest` (at most five, only modules the reader may see: a reader who may not see the modules that multiply gets nothing of them, and their set may fit), `params.chars` and `params.nodes` what its chunks expanded to, `params.kept` `true` when the room for the text kept was the one passed. Blocking, as `expansion-too-large` is: released, that commit has no chunks at all for that reader (`GET /chunks` is 422 and the change feed tells none of its chunks), worse than one module left out. A head whose base is already too large together reports it too, at modules the change set may not have touched: released, its content would have no chunks either way, so a change set does not pass review into that state unnoticed. A manual's checks do not cut the whole commit (they check a manual, not a commit).
- **Visibility:** all of it is read through `ReadableContent`, so nothing about a module the reader may not see is listed or counted (filtered before counting and paging), and `outside-scope` notes are left out. A reference to a module that has no file in any language is reported only to a reader told what was left out (`content.read.draft`, as resolving reports `missing-module`) or one from whom nothing at that commit is hidden (a role reading there whose scope restricts nothing: an author without `content.read.draft` but with an unrestricted scope sees the references they broke) (`ReadableContent.missingModule`); for anyone else a missing module and a hidden one look alike. A cross-reference to a module the reader may not see is not reported at all, so its ID never shows. Cross-references are checked in each module (the target exists, and so does the block in it) and by resolving (the target is in the manual): both name the target in `params` (`module`, `block`), so one link is one check.
- **A `Check`**: `attributes` `code` (stable), `severity` (`blocking` or `warning`, fixed per code below, but for `forbidden-term` and `style-rule`, whose severity the configuration decides), `category` (`validation`, `reference`, `image`, `attachment`, `translation`, `writing`), `subject` (a module in one language or a manual, as locks name them: where to fix it), `blockId` (the block, list item or step; null for the subject as a whole), `message` (English, for logs) and `params` (`module`, `block`, `lang`, `asset`, `at`, `blocks`: an outdated translation's block count, part of what makes it that problem, so its `id` changes with the count); `meta.variants` and `meta.languages` name the manual's variants and languages it was found in (one check per problem, however many variants show it). `meta.title` is the subject's title as the reader reads it (null when its file cannot be read). `id` is a hash of what makes it that problem, stable across requests. Ordered by subject (the manual, then modules by language and ID), the subject's own checks first, then by the block's place in the module, blocking before warnings. Kept for 30 seconds per commit and what the reader sees on it (32 at most), so paging and filtering compute them once; an image stored meanwhile shows within that. Images are looked up in the blob store eight at a time. `filter[severity]`; the document's `meta.counts` (`blocking`, `warning`) counts every check before that filter, `meta.modules` (`checked`, `withoutIssues`) the modules checked (by ID, in any language the reader may read) and how many of them no check names, `meta.commit` names the commit checked.
- **The PDF standard** (#58): with `pdf.standard` at `ua-1` (the default), a manual's checks also render every variant in every language they resolved, as the PDF route does (`PdfRenderer`, the same cache key, so the route then answers from the cache, a refusal included; one rendering at a time across all checks requests, so the checks hold at most one of the renderer's workers and the PDF routes keep the rest; at most `CHECK_RENDERS_WAITING`, 8, renderings wait for it, and a checks request finding it full, at any of its renderings, is 503 `renderer-busy` with `Retry-After`; what it rendered before is cached, so a retry goes on from there) and report each reason the renderer refuses it as a blocking `pdf-accessibility` at the manual (`params.problem`, the reason without the `PDF/UA-1 error:` prefix; `meta.variants` and `meta.languages` where it was found). A renderer under load makes the checks 503 (`renderer-busy`, try again), so the check is never left out silently; a PDF that cannot be rendered at all (too slow, a template that does not compile) is a `pdf-not-checked` warning (`params.reason`: the PDF route's error code, which says why). With `pdf.standard: none` nothing is rendered, except for a page design's layout and a callout legend beside an image (see "Page designs"). A change set's checks do the same for each manual it changes.
- **Resolve issues name their block** (`ResolveIssue.at` in `@openfolia/content-model`: the module and block an xref, a text attribute, a variant condition or a module reference was found at; `target`: what an xref links to), so a check links into the editor at its block. Since the block is part of what makes an issue distinct, a resolution now reports a missing text or variant attribute once per block rather than once per module (`meta.issues` and `X-Ccms-Issues` count them so).

| Code                                    | Severity | Category    | Means                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   |
| --------------------------------------- | -------- | ----------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `invalid-module`                        | blocking | validation  | A module file cannot be read.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |
| `invalid-manifest`                      | blocking | validation  | The manual’s manifest cannot be read.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   |
| `unknown-module-type`                   | blocking | validation  | The module’s type is not configured.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    |
| `unknown-audience`                      | blocking | validation  | The module’s or manual’s audience is not configured (a module with one is left out of every manual).                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    |
| `unknown-language`                      | blocking | validation  | The module’s language is neither the source nor a target.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |
| `unknown-facet`                         | blocking | validation  | The module names a facet that is not configured.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
| `unknown-facet-value`                   | blocking | validation  | The module names a value its facet does not have.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
| `unknown-safety-symbol`                 | blocking | validation  | A safety block uses a symbol that is not configured.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    |
| `unknown-text-attribute`                | blocking | validation  | A text attribute is not configured.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| `unknown-variant-attribute`             | blocking | validation  | A condition or variant names an attribute that is not configured.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
| `unknown-variant-value`                 | blocking | validation  | A condition or variant names a value its attribute does not have.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
| `version-compare-on-enum`               | blocking | validation  | A condition compares an attribute that is not a version.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
| `invalid-version-value`                 | blocking | validation  | A version attribute’s value is not a version.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |
| `missing-variant-attribute`             | blocking | validation  | A published variant does not set an attribute a condition tests: the content under it is left out.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| `missing-text-attribute`                | blocking | validation  | A published variant has no value for a text attribute the content uses.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| `pdf-accessibility`                     | blocking | validation  | A variant’s PDF in a language cannot meet PDF/UA-1, the configured PDF standard (`params.problem` says why).                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |
| `pdf-not-checked`                       | warning  | validation  | A variant’s PDF in a language could not be rendered (too slow, a broken template): PDF/UA-1 was not checked.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |
